# **Persecution of Uyghurs in China**

Since 2014, the [government of the People's Republic of](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_the_People%27s_Republic_of_China) [China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_the_People%27s_Republic_of_China) has committed a series of ongoing [human rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights#Violations) [abuses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights#Violations) against [Uyghurs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uyghurs) and other [Turkic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkic_peoples) Muslim minorities in [Xinjiang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xinjiang) which has often been characterized as [persecution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persecution) or as [genocide.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genocide) There have been reports of mass [arbitrary arrests and detention,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arbitrary_arrest_and_detention) [torture,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torture) [mass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_surveillance) [surveillance,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_surveillance) cultural and religious persecution, [family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Family_separation) [separation,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Family_separation) [forced labor,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forced_labor) [sexual violence,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_violence) and violations of [reproductive rights.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reproductive_rights)

In 2014, the [administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xi_Jinping_Administration) of [Chinese Communist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Communist_Party) (CCP) [general secretary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Secretary_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party) [Xi Jinping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xi_Jinping) launched the [Strike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strike_Hard_Campaign_Against_Violent_Terrorism) [Hard Campaign Against Violent Terrorism,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strike_Hard_Campaign_Against_Violent_Terrorism) which involved surveillance and restrictions in Xinjiang[.\[2\]](#page-30-0) Beginning in 2017, under Xinjiang [CCP Secretary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Communist_Party_Committee_Secretary) [Chen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chen_Quanguo) [Quanguo,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chen_Quanguo)[\[3\]](#page-30-1) the government incarcerated over an estimated one million Uyghurs without [legal process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legal_process) in [internment camps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xinjiang_internment_camps) officially described as "vocational education and training centers", in the largest mass internment of an ethnic-religious minority group since [World War II.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_II)[\[4\]](#page-31-0)[\[5\]](#page-31-1) China began to wind down the camps in 2019, and [Amnesty International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amnesty_International) states that detainees have been increasingly transferred to the [penal system.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penal_system_in_China)

In addition to mass detention, government policies have included [forced labor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forced_labor) and factory work,[\[6\]](#page-31-2)[\[7\]](#page-31-3) suppression of Uyghur [religious practices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_China)[,\[8\]](#page-31-4) political [indoctrination,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indoctrination) [\[9\]](#page-31-5) [forced sterilization,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forced_sterilization)[\[10\]](#page-31-6) forced [contraception](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Contraception)[,\[11\]](#page-31-7)[\[12\]](#page-31-8) and [forced abortion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forced_abortion)[.\[13\]](#page-32-0)[\[14\]](#page-32-1) An estimated 16,000 [mosques](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mosque) have been razed or damaged[,\[3\]](#page-30-1) and hundreds of thousands of children have been forcibly separated from their parents and sent to [boarding schools.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boarding_schools_in_China)[\[15\]](#page-32-2)[\[16\]](#page-32-3) Chinese government statistics reported that from 2015 to 2018, [birth rates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birth_rate) in the mostly Uyghur regions of [Hotan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hotan) and [Kashgar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashgar) fell by more than 60%[.\[10\]](#page-31-6) In the same period, the birth rate of the whole country decreased by 9.7%.[\[17\]](#page-32-4) Chinese authorities according to CNN acknowledged that birth rates dropped by almost a third in 2018 in Xinjiang,

### **Background**

#### **Uyghur identity**

Uyghurs are a [Turkic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkic_peoples) ethnic group native to [Xinjiang.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xinjiang) They are distinct from the [Han Chinese,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Han_Chinese) the predominant ethnic group in China.[\[40\]](#page-35-6) Uyghurs are the second-largest predominantly Muslim ethnicity in China, after the [Hui,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hui_people) and [Sunni Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni_Islam) is an important aspect of Uyghur identity.[\[40\]](#page-35-6) The [Uyghur language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uyghur_language) has around 10 million speakers and is shared with other minority groups in the region[.\[41\]](#page-35-7)

#### **Xinjiang conflict**

Both Uyghurs and the predominantly Han government lay claim to Xinjiang.[\[42\]](#page-35-8) This prompted an [ethnic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_conflict) [con](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_conflict)flict featuring resistance and sporadic violence as Uyghurs sought greater autonomy[.\[43\]](#page-35-9) Sinologists Anna Hayes and Michael Clarke have described Xinjiang as undergoing a process of transition as the Chinese government attempted to transform it from a frontier region to an "integral" province of a unitary Chinese state.[\[44\]](#page-36-0)

#### **Imperial China**

Historically, certain [Chinese dynasties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dynasties_in_Chinese_history) exerted control over parts of modern-day Xinjiang[.\[45\]](#page-36-1) The region fully came under Chinese rule as a result of the [westward expansion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dzungar%E2%80%93Qing_Wars) of the [Manchu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manchu_people)led [Qing dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qing_dynasty) during the 1700s, which also saw the [conquests of Tibet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tibet_under_Qing_rule) and [Mongolia.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongolia_under_Qing_rule)[\[46\]](#page-36-2) Xinjiang was a peripheral part of the [Qing empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qing_dynasty) and briefly regained independence during the [Dungan Revolt \(1862–1877\).](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dungan_Revolt_(1862%E2%80%931877))[\[47\]](#page-36-3) The Uyghur population participated in the [Dzungar genocide,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dzungar_genocide)

## ![](_page_2_Picture_5.jpeg)

A Uyghur man from [Kashgar,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashgar) a city in [Xinjiang,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xinjiang) China.

resulting in the [Qianlong emperor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qianlong_emperor) granting them permission to resettle in the former territories of [Dzungaria.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dzungaria) [\[48\]](#page-36-4)[\[49\]](#page-36-5)

#### **Republican Era (1912–1949)**

The region was semi-autonomous during the [Republic of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_China_(1912%E2%80%931949))'s [Warlord Era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warlord_Era) (1916–1928), with parts controlled by the [Kumul Khanate,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kumul_Khanate) the [Ma clique](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ma_clique) and later the warlord [Jin Shuren.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jin_Shuren)[\[50\]](#page-36-6) In 1933, the breakaway [First East Turkestan Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_East_Turkestan_Republic) was established in the [Kumul Rebellion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kumul_Rebellion)[,\[51\]](#page-36-7) but was conquered the following year by warlord [Sheng Shicai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheng_Shicai) with the help of the [Soviet Union.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_invasion_of_Xinjiang)[\[52\]](#page-36-8) In 1944, the [Ili Rebellion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ili_Rebellion) led to the establishment of the [Second East Turkestan Republic,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_East_Turkestan_Republic) which was dependent on the Soviet Union until it was [absorbed into the People's Republic of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Incorporation_of_Xinjiang_into_the_People%27s_Republic_of_China) in 1949.[\[53\]](#page-36-9)

#### **People's Republic of China (1949–present)**

## ![](_page_2_Figure_11.jpeg)

Present day map of Xinjiang and its internal and external borders

From the 1950s to the 1970s, the Chinese government sponsored [a mass migration of Han Chinese to Xinjiang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Migration_to_Xinjiang) and introduced policies designed to suppress cultural identity and religion in the region.[\[54\]](#page-36-10) During this period, Uyghur independence organizations emerged with some support from the Soviet Union, with the [East Turkestan People's Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Turkestan_People%27s_Revolutionary_Party) being the largest in 1968. [\[55\]](#page-36-11) During the 1970s, the Soviets supported the [United](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Revolutionary_Front_of_East_Turkestan) [Revolutionary Front of East Turkestan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Revolutionary_Front_of_East_Turkestan) (URFET) against the Han Chinese.[\[56\]](#page-36-12)

During the 1980s under [Deng Xiaoping,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deng_Xiaoping) the PRC pursued a new policy of cultural liberalization in Xinjiang and adopted a flexible language policy nationally[.\[57\]](#page-36-13) Despite a positive response among party officials and minority groups, the Chinese government viewed this policy as unsuccessful and from the mid-1980s its official pluralistic language policy became increasingly subordinate to a covert policy of minority assimilation motivated by geopolitical concerns[.\[58\]](#page-37-0)

Consequently, and in Xinjiang particularly, multilingualism and cultural pluralism were restricted to favor a "monolingual, monocultural model", which in turn helped to embed and strengthen an oppositional Uyghur identity[.\[59\]](#page-37-1) Attempts by the Chinese state to encourage economic development in the region by exploiting natural resources led to ethnic tension and discontent within Xinjiang over the region's lack of autonomy[.\[60\]](#page-37-2) In April 1990, a [violent uprising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baren_Township_conflict) in [Barin,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barin_Township) near Kashgar, was suppressed by the [People's Liberation Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People%27s_Liberation_Army) (PLA), involving a large number of deaths[.\[60\]](#page-37-2)[\[3\]](#page-30-1)[\[61\]](#page-37-3) Writing in 1998, political scientist [Barry Sautman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barry_Sautman) considered policies designed to reduce inequality between Han Chinese and ethnic minorities in Xinjiang unsuccessful at eliminating conflicts because they were shaped by the "paternalistic and hierarchical approach to ethnic relations adopted by the Chinese government"[.\[62\]](#page-37-4)

In February 1997, a police roundup and execution of suspected "separatists" during [Ramadan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramadan) led to large demonstrations, which led to a PLA crackdown on protesters resulting in at least nine deaths in what became known as the [Ghulja incident.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghulja_incident)[\[63\]](#page-37-5)[\[64\]](#page-37-6)[\[65\]](#page-37-7) The [Ürümqi bus bombings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1997_%C3%9Cr%C3%BCmqi_bus_bombings) later that month killed nine people and injured 68, with Uyghur exile groups claiming responsibility.[\[66\]](#page-37-8) In March 1997, a bus bomb killed two people, with responsibility claimed by Uyghur separatists and the [Turkey-](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkey)based "Organisation for East Turkistan Freedom"[.\[67\]](#page-37-9)

The [July 2009 Ürümqi riots,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/July_2009_%C3%9Cr%C3%BCmqi_riots) which resulted in over one hundred deaths, broke out in response to the [Shaoguan incident,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shaoguan_incident) a violent dispute between Uyghur and Han Chinese factory workers.[\[68\]](#page-38-0) Following the riots, Uyghur terrorists killed dozens of Han Chinese in coordinated attacks from 2009 to 2016.[\[69\]](#page-38-1)[\[70\]](#page-38-2) These included the [September 2009 Xinjiang unrest,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/September_2009_Xinjiang_unrest)[\[71\]](#page-38-3) the [2011 Hotan attack,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_Hotan_attack)[\[72\]](#page-38-4) the [2014 Kunming attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Kunming_attack)[,\[73\]](#page-38-5) the [April 2014 Ürümqi attack,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/April_2014_%C3%9Cr%C3%BCmqi_attack)[\[74\]](#page-38-6) and the [May 2014 Ürümqi attack.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/May_2014_%C3%9Cr%C3%BCmqi_attack)[\[75\]](#page-38-7) The attacks were conducted by Uyghur separatists, with some orchestrated by the [Turkistan Islamic Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkistan_Islamic_Party) (a UN-designated terrorist organization, formerly called the East Turkistan Islamic Movement).[\[76\]](#page-39-0)

## **Government policies**

### **Initial "Strike Hard Campaign Against Violent Terrorism"**

During the run-up to the [2008 Beijing Olympics,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_Beijing_Olympics) the Chinese state began to emphasize *[weiwen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weiwen)* (stability maintenance) which led to an intensification of [repression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_repression) across the country. Some within the Party warned that increased action to combat instability which might not even exist could lead to a spiral of repression and unrest[.\[3\]](#page-30-1)

## ![](_page_3_Figure_8.jpeg)

## Xinjiang police job advertisements by year

In April 2010, after the July 2009 Ürümqi riots, [Zhang Chunxian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhang_Chunxian) replaced the former CCP [secretary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Party_Committee_Secretary) [Wang Lequan,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wang_Lequan) who had been behind religious policies in Xinjiang for 14 years.[\[77\]](#page-39-1) Following the unrest, party theorists began to call for implementing a more monocultural society with a single "state-race" which would allow China to become "a new type of superpower".

Policies to further this goal were first implemented by Zhang Chunxian. Following [an attack in Yunnan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Kunming_attack)

[Province,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Kunming_attack) Xi Jinping told the politburo "We should unite the people to build a copper and iron wall against terrorism", and "Make terrorists like rats scurrying across the street, with everybody shouting, 'Beat them!' " In April 2014, Xi traveled to Xinjiang and told police in Kashgar that "We must be as harsh as them, and show absolutely no mercy." A suicide bombing occurred in Ürümqi on the last day of his visit[.\[3\]](#page-30-1)

In 2014, a secret meeting of CCP leadership was held in Beijing to find a solution to the problem, which would become known as the [Strike Hard Campaign Against Violent Terrorism.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strike_Hard_Campaign_Against_Violent_Terrorism)[\[3\]](#page-30-1) In May 2014, China publicly launched the campaign in Xinjiang in

## ![](_page_4_Figure_4.jpeg)

Number of "re-education" related government procurement bids in Xinjiang response to growing tensions between the Han Chinese and the Uyghur populations of Xinjiang[.\[78\]](#page-39-2)[\[79\]](#page-39-3) In announcing the campaign, CCP general secretary Xi Jinping stated that "practice has proved that our party's ruling strategy in Xinjiang is correct and must be maintained in the long run".[\[80\]](#page-39-4)

In 2016, there was a brief window of opportunity for Uyghurs with passports to leave China; many did so but had to leave relatives and children without passports behind. Many of these families have not been reunited. [\[81\]](#page-39-5)

Following guidance from Beijing, CCP leadership in Xinjiang commenced a ["People's War"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People%27s_War) against the "Three Evil Forces" of separatism, terrorism, and extremism. They deployed two hundred thousand party cadres to Xinjiang and launched the [Civil Servant-Family Pair Up](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Servant-Family_Pair_Up) program. Xi was dissatisfied with the initial results of the People's War and replaced Zhang Chunxian with [Chen Quanguo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chen_Quanguo) in 2016. Following his appointment Chen oversaw the recruitment of tens of thousands of additional police officers and the division of society into three categories: trusted, average, and untrustworthy. He instructed his subordinates to "Take this crackdown as the top project", and "to preempt the enemy, to strike at the outset".[\[3\]](#page-30-1)

### **Regulations since 2017**

Following a meeting with Xi in Beijing, Chen Quanguo held a rally in Ürümqi with ten thousand troops, helicopters, and armored vehicles. As they paraded, he announced a "smashing, obliterating offensive", and declared that they would "bury the corpses of terrorists and terror gangs in the vast sea of the People's War." He ordered them to "Round up everyone who should be rounded up", and by April 2017 mass arrests had begun[.\[3\]](#page-30-1) New bans and regulations were implemented on 1 April 2017. Abnormally long beards and the wearing of veils in public were both banned. Not watching state-run television or listening to radio broadcasts, refusing to abide by family planning policies, or refusing to allow one's children to attend staterun schools were all prohibited[.\[82\]](#page-39-6)

In 2017, China's Ministry of Public Security began to procure race-based monitoring systems which could reportedly identify whether or not an individual was Uyghur. Despite its questionable accuracy, this allowed a "Uyghur alarm" to be added to surveillance systems. Enhanced border controls were also implemented with guilt being presumed in the absence of evidence. Zhu Hailun, Communist Party secretary of Kashgar, signed off on a bulletin in 2017 that presumed guilt in persons from Xinjing who had travelled abroad. According to Zhu, "If suspected terrorism cannot be ruled out, then a border control should be implemented to insure the person's arrest".[\[3\]](#page-30-1)

In 2017, 73% of foreign journalists in China reported being restricted or prohibited from reporting in Xinjiang, up from 42% in 2016.[\[83\]](#page-39-7)

Alleged "re-education" efforts began in 2014 and were expanded in 2017[.\[84\]](#page-40-0)[\[85\]](#page-40-1) Internment camps were built for the housing of students of the "re-education" programs, most of whom were Uyghurs. Chen ordered that the camps "be managed like the military and defended like a prison"[.\[3\]](#page-30-1) The Chinese government did not acknowledge the camps' existence until 2018 and called them "vocational education and training centers"[.\[84\]](#page-40-0) [\[86\]](#page-40-2) From 2019, the government began referring to them as "vocational training centers". The camps tripled in size from 2018 to 2019 despite the Chinese government stating that most of the detainees had been released. [\[84\]](#page-40-0)

The use of these centers appears to have ended in 2019 following international pressure[.\[87\]](#page-40-3) Academic Kerry Brown attributes their closures beginning in late 2019 to the expense required to operate them[.\[88\]](#page-40-4) Although no comprehensive independent surveys of such centers have been performed as of October 2022, spot checks by journalists have found such sites converted or abandoned.[\[87\]](#page-40-3) In 2022, a Washington Post reporter checked a dozen sites previously identified as re-education centers and found "[m]ost of them appeared to be empty or converted, with several sites labeled as coronavirus quarantine facilities, teachers' schools and vocational schools."[\[87\]](#page-40-3)

#### **Propaganda campaign**

The Chinese government has engaged in a [propaganda campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Propaganda) to defend its actions in Xinjiang[.\[89\]](#page-40-5) China initially denied the existence of the Xinjiang internment camps and attempted to [cover up](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cover-up) their existence.[\[90\]](#page-40-6) In 2018, after widespread reporting forced it to admit that the Xinjiang internment camps exist, the Chinese government initiated a campaign to portray the camps as humane and to deny that human rights abuses occurred in Xinjiang.[\[91\]](#page-40-7) In 2020 and 2021, the propaganda campaign expanded due to rising international backlash against government policies[,\[92\]](#page-41-0) with the Chinese government worrying that it no longer had control of the narrative.[\[90\]](#page-40-6)

Chinese authorities have responded to allegations of abuse by Uyghur women by mounting character attacks. This included the disclosure of confidential medical and personal information in an attempt to slander witnesses and undermine their testimony.[\[93\]](#page-41-1) The goal of these attacks appeared to be to silence further criticism, rather than to refute specific claims made by critics[.\[94\]](#page-41-2) Presentations given by Xinjiang's publicity department and the [Ministry of Foreign A](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Foreign_Affairs_of_the_People%27s_Republic_of_China)ffairs to dispel allegations of abuse are closed to foreign journalists and feature pre-recorded questions as well as pre-recorded monologues from people in Xinjiang, including relatives of witnesses.[\[93\]](#page-41-1)

Chinese government propaganda attacks have also targeted international journalists covering human rights abuses in Xinjiang[.\[95\]](#page-41-3)[\[96\]](#page-41-4)[\[97\]](#page-41-5) After providing coverage critical of Chinese government abuses in Xinjiang, [BBC News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BBC_News) reporter [John Sudworth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Sudworth) was subjected to a campaign of propaganda and harassment by Chinese state-affiliated and CCP-affiliated media.[\[95\]](#page-41-3)[\[98\]](#page-41-6)[\[99\]](#page-41-7) The public attacks resulted in Sudworth and his wife [Yvonne Murray,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yvonne_Murray_(journalist)) who reports for [Raidió Teilifís Éireann,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raidi%C3%B3_Teilif%C3%ADs_%C3%89ireann) fleeing China for [Taiwan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwan) fearing for their safety.[\[98\]](#page-41-6) [\[100\]](#page-42-0)

The Chinese government has used social media as a part of its propaganda campaign.[\[101\]](#page-42-1)[\[102\]](#page-42-2) The government purchased [Facebook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Facebook) advertisements to spread propaganda designed to incite doubt on the existence and scope of human rights violations occurring within Xinjiang.[\[103\]](#page-42-3)[\[104\]](#page-42-4)[\[105\]](#page-42-5) [Douyin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Douyin) presents its users with Chinese state propaganda pertaining to the human rights abuses in Xinjiang[.\[102\]](#page-42-2)[\[106\]](#page-42-6)[\[107\]](#page-42-7) Between July 2019 and early August 2019, [CCP-](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Communist_Party)owned tabloid the *[Global Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Times)* paid [Twitter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twitter) to promote tweets that denied that the Chinese government was committing human rights abuses in Xinjiang; Twitter later banned advertising from state-controlled media outlets on August 19 after removing large numbers of [pro-Beijing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twitter_bot) [bots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twitter_bot) from the social network[.\[108\]](#page-43-0)[\[109\]](#page-43-1)

In April 2021, the Chinese government released 5 propaganda videos titled, "Xinjiang is a Wonderful Land", and released a musical titled "The Wings of Songs" which portrayed Xinjiang as harmonious and peaceful. [\[110\]](#page-43-2)[\[111\]](#page-43-3)[\[112\]](#page-43-4) The Wings of Songs portrays "a rural idyll of ethnic cohesion devoid of repression, mass surveillance" and without Islam[.\[113\]](#page-43-5)

In June 2021, [Pro Publica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pro Publica) documented a Chinese government-backed propaganda campaign on Twitter and [You Tube](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/You Tube) involving more than 5000 videos analysed. The videos showed Uyghurs in Xinjiang denying abuses and scolding foreign officials and [multinational corporations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multinational_corporation) who had questioned China's human rights record in the province. Some of the videos' accounts were removed on You Tube as part of You Tube's efforts to combat [spam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spamming) and influence operations.[\[114\]](#page-43-6)

In October 2022, the [Australian Strategic Policy Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Strategic_Policy_Institute) documented a number of CCP-backed Uyghur influencers in Xinjiang posting propaganda videos on Chinese and Western social media which pushed back against abuse allegations. Some of the influencers' accounts were suspended on Twitter for alleged inauthenticity[.\[115\]](#page-43-7)

On 30 October 2023, the Chinese embassy in France posted a photo on [X](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/X_(website)) comparing the buildings in Xinjiang, which were standing intact, with buildings in Gaza that had been destroyed in the [Gaza war.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaza_war)[\[116\]](#page-43-8) East Turkestan Government in Exile leader [Salih Hudayar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salih_Hudayar) and Uyghur lawyer [Rayhan Asat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rayhan_Asat) criticized the photo as propaganda and argued that China's crackdown was more pervasive than the situation in Gaza.[\[116\]](#page-43-8) [\[117\]](#page-44-0)

#### **Counter-terrorism justification**

China has used the global ["war on terror"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_on_terror) of the 2000s to frame "separatist" and ethnic unrest as acts of Islamist terrorism to legitimize its policies in Xinjiang[.\[118\]](#page-44-1) Scholars such as Sean Roberts and David Tobin have described [Islamophobia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamophobia) and fear of terrorism as discourses that have been used within China to justify repressive policies targeting Uyghurs, arguing that violence against Uyghurs should be seen in the context of [Chinese colonialism,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_expansionism) rather than exclusively as a part of an anti-terrorism campaign.[\[119\]](#page-44-2) According to academic David Tobin, since 2012, "Chinese education about Uyghurs tends to frame Uyghur identities as racialised, culturally external existential threats to be defeated by state violence or teaching them to be Chinese."[\[120\]](#page-44-3)

[Arienne Dwyer,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arienne_Dwyer) Professor Emerita at [University of Kansas,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Kansas) has written that the US war on terror gave China an opportunity to characterise and "conflate" Uyghur nationalism with terrorism, particularity through the use of [state-run media.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_media_in_China) Dwyer argues that the influence of fundamentalist forms of Islam such as [Sala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salafism)fism within Xinjiang is overstated by China as it is tempered by Uyghur Sufi[sm.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sufism)[\[57\]](#page-36-13)

In December 2015, the Associated Press reported that China had effectively expelled [Ursula Gauthier,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ursula_Gauthier) a French journalist, "for questioning the official line equating ethnic violence in the western Muslim region with global terrorism".[\[121\]](#page-44-4) Gauthier, who was the first foreign journalist forced to leave China since 2012, was subjected to what the AP described as an "abusive and intimidating campaign" by Chinese state media that accused her of "having [hurt the feelings of the Chinese people"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurt_the_feelings_of_the_Chinese_people) and that a Chinese Foreign Ministry spokesman accused her of emboldening terrorism[.\[121\]](#page-44-4)

In August 2018, the [United Nations Committee on the Elimination of Racial Discrimination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Committee_on_the_Elimination_of_Racial_Discrimination) decried the "broad definition of terrorism and vague references to extremism" used by Chinese legislation, noting that there were numerous reports of detention of large numbers of ethnic Uyghurs and other Muslim minorities on the "pretext of countering terrorism".[\[122\]](#page-44-5)

In 2019, [Sam Brownback,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sam_Brownback) [Nathan Sales,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nathan_Sales) and the editorial board of *[The Wall Street Journal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Wall_Street_Journal)* each said that the Chinese government consistently misused "counterterrorism" as a pretext for cultural suppression and human rights abuses[.\[123\]](#page-44-6)[\[124\]](#page-45-0)

In 2021, [Shirzat Bawudun,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shirzat_Bawudun) the former head of the Xinjiang department of justice, and [Sattar Sawut,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sattar_Sawut) the former head of the Xinjiang education department, were sentenced to [death with two years reprieve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_sentence_with_reprieve) on terrorism and separatism charges[.\[125\]](#page-45-1)[\[126\]](#page-45-2) Three other educators and two textbook editors were given lesser sentences[.\[127\]](#page-45-3) One of the Uyghur textbook editors, [Yalqun Rozi,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yalqun_Rozi) received a 15-year prison sentence[.\[128\]](#page-45-4) His son, Kamaltürk Yalqun, speculated that the reason was that the textbooks his father had edited were "rich in Uyghur culture", and that the government is trying to eliminate that culture and to "erase history and write a new narrative"[.\[129\]](#page-45-5)

### **Human rights abuses**

#### **Inside internment camps**

#### **Mass detention**

Especially since 2016, [internment camps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xinjiang_internment_camps) have been a part of the Chinese government's strategy to govern Xinjian[g\[130\]](#page-45-6) through the detention of ethnic minorities *en masse*. [\[131\]](#page-45-7) According to [Adrian Zenz,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adrian_Zenz) a researcher on the camps, the mass internments peaked in 2018 and have abated since then, with officials shifting focus towards forced labor programs[.\[132\]](#page-46-0) In September 2023, Amnesty International said that they were "witnessing more and more arbitrary detention", but that detained individuals were being moved from the camps into [Chinese "formal prisons".](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penal_system_in_China)[\[133\]](#page-46-1) As of April 2024, the Uyghur Human Rights Project estimated that China had imprisoned 449,000 Uyghurs or about one in 17[.\[134\]](#page-46-2)

In 2021, [CNN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CNN) published an interview with a former Xinjiang police officer who said that when the police planned to raid a Uyghur village, they would sometimes arrange for the entire village to gather for a meeting with their chief so that the police could show up and arrest everyone. On other occasions the police would go door-to-door with rifles and pull all the residents from their homes overnight. Once the police had arrested people, they would interrogate and beat every man, woman, and child over age 14 until they kneeled on the floor and cried[.\[135\]](#page-46-3)

Researchers and organizations have made various estimates of the number of Xinjiang internment camp detainees. In 2018, [United Nations Committee on the Elimination of Racial Discrimination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Committee_on_the_Elimination_of_Racial_Discrimination) vice chairperson Gay Mc Dougall indicated that around 1 million Uyghurs were being held in internment camps[.\[136\]](#page-46-4) While Mc Dougall did not cite sources for her statement, it was consistent with a report submitted to the committee by the Network of Chinese Human Rights Defenders*.* [\[136\]](#page-46-4) Other estimates submitted to the committee were more careful, with Human Rights Watch estimating at least tens of thousands and Amnesty International estimating tens of thousands to hundreds of thousands of detainees[.\[136\]](#page-46-4)

In March 2019, [Adrian Zenz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adrian_Zenz) told the [United Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations) that 1.5 million Uyghurs had been detained in camps, saying that the number accounted for the increases in the size and scope of detention in the region and public reporting on the stories of Uyghur exiles with family in internment camps[.\[137\]](#page-46-5) In July 2019, Zenz wrote in a paper published by the *Journal of Political Risk* that 1.5 million Uyghurs had been extrajudicially detained, which he described as being "an equivalent to just under one in six adult members of a Turkic and predominantly Muslim minority group in Xinjiang."[\[138\]](#page-46-6) In November 2019, Zenz estimated that the number of internment camps in Xinjiang had surpassed 1,000.[\[139\]](#page-47-0) In July 2020, Zenz wrote in *[Foreign Policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_Policy)* that his estimate had increased since November 2019, estimating that a total of 1.8 million Uyghurs and other Muslim minorities had been extrajudicially detained in what he described as "the largest incarceration of an ethnoreligious minority since the Holocaust", arguing that the Chinese Government was engaging in policies in violation of the United Nations Convention on the Prevention and Punishment of the Crime of Genocide. [\[140\]](#page-47-1) In September 2020, a Chinese government white paper stated that an average of 1.29 million workers went through "vocational training" per year between 2014 and 2019, though it does not specify how many of the people received the training in camps or how many times they went through training. [Adrian Zenz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adrian_Zenz) stated that this "gives us a possible scope of coercive labor" occurring in Xinjiang[.\[141\]](#page-47-2)

According to a 2020 study by Joanne Smith Finley, "political re-education involves coercive Sinicization, deaths in the camps through malnutrition, unsanitary conditions, withheld medical care, and violence (beatings); rape of male and female prisoners; and, since the end of 2018, transfers of the most recalcitrant prisoners – usually young, religious males – to high-security prisons in Xinjiang or inner China. Other camp 'graduates' have been sent into securitized forced labour. Those who remain outside the camps have been terrified into religious and cultural self-censorship through the threat of internment.["\[4\]](#page-31-0)

#### **Deaths**

There have been numerous reports since 2018 of Uyghurs dying in detention, and of former detainees dying as a result of injuries sustained in custody. [Human Rights Watch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Rights_Watch) stated that as of April 2021 there had been at least 177 such deaths, and that the Chinese government often refused to acknowledge them[.\[142\]](#page-47-3) Other deaths have reportedly occurred through starvation and refusal to provide medical care.[\[4\]](#page-31-0) According to the [New](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Lines_Institute_for_Strategy_and_Policy) [Lines Institute for Strategy and Policy,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Lines_Institute_for_Strategy_and_Policy) Uyghur religious figures are more likely to die or disappear in custody than other detainees[.\[143\]](#page-47-4)

There have been several reports of mass deaths of prisoners in the internment camps[.\[144\]](#page-47-5)[\[145\]](#page-47-6) In 2019 [Radio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_Free_Asia) [Free Asia,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_Free_Asia) a United States government-funded broadcaster, reported that a Chinese police officer who previously served as a camp administrative assistant had confirmed that 150 Uyghurs had died at the No. 1 internment camp in [Kuqa County.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuqa,_Xinjiang)[\[146\]](#page-47-7) Former detainee [Mihrigul Tursun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mihrigul_Tursun) claimed that nine women had died in one camp during the three months she was held there, which she attributed to the poor conditions they were held in.[\[147\]](#page-48-0)

American researcher [Ethan Gutmann](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethan_Gutmann) estimated in December 2020 that 5 to 10 percent of detainees had died each year in the camps[.\[148\]](#page-48-1) Russian-American scholar Gene Buni[n\[149\]](#page-48-2) created the [Xinjiang Victims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xinjiang_Victims_Database) [Database](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xinjiang_Victims_Database) which had documented 12,050 victims in April 2021,[\[150\]](#page-48-3) and 225 deaths for those serving official prison sentences as of November 2023[.\[151\]](#page-48-4) The database drew ridicule online after it included photos of Hong Kong actors [Andy Lau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andy_Lau) and [Chow Yun-fat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chow_Yun-fat) in a list of police officers allegedly responsible for the crackdowns[.\[152\]](#page-49-0)[\[153\]](#page-49-1)[\[154\]](#page-49-2)

In 2021, a former Xinjiang police officer told [Sky News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sky_News) that imprisoned Uyghurs were frequently beaten to death by interrogators during his service.[\[155\]](#page-49-3)

#### **Torture**

Rights groups and others have reported that Uyghurs living in Xinjiang have been subjected to torture by authorities[.\[156\]](#page-50-0)[\[157\]](#page-50-1)[\[158\]](#page-50-2) A former Chinese police detective, exiled in Europe, revealed to CNN in 2021 details of the systematic torture of Uyghurs in detention camps in Xinjiang, acts in which he had participated, and the fear of his own arrest had he dissented while in China.[\[135\]](#page-46-3)[\[159\]](#page-50-3)

[Mihrigul Tursun,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mihrigul_Tursun) a young Uyghur mother, said that she was "tortured and subjected to other brutal conditions."[\[160\]](#page-50-4) In 2018, Tursun gave an intervie[w\[161\]](#page-50-5) [\[162\]](#page-50-6) during which she described her experience while at the camps; she was drugged, interrogated for days

## ![](_page_9_Picture_8.jpeg)

[Mihrigul Tursun,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mihrigul_Tursun) a former detainee of the [Xinjiang internment camps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xinjiang_internment_camps)

without sleep, subjected to intrusive medical examinations, and strapped in a chair and [received electric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electrocution) [shocks.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electrocution) It was her third time being sent to a camp since 2015. Tursun told reporters that she remembered interrogators tell her "Being a Uighur is a crime.["\[160\]](#page-50-4) Chinese Foreign Ministry spokesperson [Hua Chunying](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hua_Chunying) stated that Tursun was taken into custody by police on "suspicion of inciting ethnic hatred and discrimination" for a period lasting 20 days and denied that Tursun was ever detained in an internment camp. [\[162\]](#page-50-6)[\[163\]](#page-51-0)[\[164\]](#page-51-1)

Another past detainee, Kayrat Samarkand, described wearing "what they called 'iron clothes,' a suit made of metal that weighed over 50 pounds [23 kg]... It forced my arms and legs into an outstretched position. I couldn't move at all, and my back was in terrible pain... They made people wear this thing to break their spirits. After 12 hours, I became so soft, quiet and lawful."[\[165\]](#page-51-2)

[Waterboarding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waterboarding) is reportedly among the forms of torture which have been used as part of the indoctrination process[.\[166\]](#page-51-3)

#### **Compulsory sterilizations and contraception**

In 2019, reports of forced sterilization in Xinjiang began to surface[.\[167\]](#page-51-4)[\[168\]](#page-51-5)[\[169\]](#page-51-6) Zumrat Dwut, a Uyghur woman, says that she was [forcibly sterilized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Compulsory_sterilization) by [tubal ligation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tubal_ligation) during her time in a camp before her husband was able to get her out through requests to Pakistani diplomats[.\[18\]](#page-32-5)[\[170\]](#page-52-0) The Xinjiang regional government denies that she was forcibly sterilized[.\[18\]](#page-32-5) Sayragul Sauytbay, an ethnic [Kazakh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakhs) teacher who later fled China, said that rape and torture were commonplace and that authorities forced detainees to take a medicine that left some individuals sterile or cognitively impaired.[\[171\]](#page-52-1)

In 2020, the Associated Press interviewed seven former detainees from internment camps who said they had been forced to take birth control pills or injected with fluids without explanation, which caused women to stop getting periods. The AP suggested the fluid may have been the hormonal medication [Depo-Provera,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medroxyprogesterone_acetate) which is commonly used in Xinjiang hospitals for birth control[.\[10\]](#page-31-6)

In April 2021, exiled Uyghur doctor Gülgine reported that forced sterilization of ethnic Uyghurs persisted since the 1980s.[\[172\]](#page-52-2) Since 2014, there was an indication for a sharp increase in sterilization of Uyghur women to ensure that Uyghurs would remain a minority in the region[.\[172\]](#page-52-2) Gülgine said "On some days there were about 80 surgeries to carry out forced sterilizations". She presented intrauterine devices (IUDs) and remarked that "these devices were inserted into women's wombs" to forcibly cause infertility[.\[172\]](#page-52-2)

#### **Indoctrination**

Former detainee Kayrat Samarkand described his camp routine in an article for [NPR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NPR) in 2018: "In addition to living in cramped quarters, he says inmates had to sing songs praising Chinese leader Xi Jinping before being allowed to eat. He says detainees were forced to memorize a list of what he calls '126 lies' about religion: 'Religion is [opium,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opium_of_the_people) religion is bad, you must believe in no religion, you must believe in the Communist Party,' he remembers. 'Only [the] Communist Party could lead you to the bright future.' "[\[165\]](#page-51-2)

The [American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States) far-right [Heritage Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heritage_Foundation) claimed that "children whose parents are detained in the camps are often sent to state-run [orphanages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orphanage) and [brainwashed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brainwashing) to forget their ethnic roots. Even if their parents are not detained, Uyghur children need to move to inner China and immerse themselves into the Han culture under the Chinese government's 'Xinjiang classrooms' policy."[\[173\]](#page-52-3)

In 2021, Gulbahar Haitiwaji reported being coerced into denouncing her family after her daughter was photographed at a protest in Paris.[\[174\]](#page-52-4)

#### **Forced labor**

According to *Quartz*, the Xinjiang region is described by the [Uyghur Human Rights Project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uyghur_Human_Rights_Project) as a " 'cotton [gulag'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulag) where prison labor is present in all steps of the cotton supply chain...["\[175\]](#page-52-5) Tahir Hamut, a Uyghur, worked in a [labor camp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labor_camp) during [elementary school](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elementary_school) when he was a child, and he later worked in a [labor camp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Re-education_through_labor) as an adult, performing tasks such as picking cotton, shoveling gravel, and making bricks. He said: "Everyone is forced to do all types of hard labor or face punishment. Anyone unable to complete their duties will be beaten."[\[176\]](#page-52-6)

*[Buzz Feed News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buzz Feed_News)* reported in December 2020 that "[f]orced labor on a vast scale is almost certainly taking place" inside the Xinjiang internment camps, with 135 factory facilities identified within the camps covering over 21 million square feet (2.0 km2) of land.[\[177\]](#page-52-7) The report noted that "[f]ourteen million square feet of new factories were built in 2018 alone" within the camps and that "former detainees said they were never given a choice about working, and that they earned a pittance or no pay at all".[\[177\]](#page-52-7)

A Chinese website hosted by [Baidu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baidu) has posted job listings for transferring Uyghur laborers in batches of 50 to 100 people[.\[178\]](#page-53-0) The 2019 Five Year Plan of the Xinjiang government has an official "labour transfer programme" "to provide more employment opportunities for the surplus rural labour force".[\[178\]](#page-53-0) These batches of Uyghurs are under "half-military" style management and direct supervision. A seafood processing plant owner said that the Uyghur workforce in his factory had left for Xinjiang due to the [COVID-19](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic) [pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic) and were paid and housed properly.[\[178\]](#page-53-0) At least 83 companies were found to have profited from Uyghur labor. Company responses included pledges of ensuring that it does not happen again by checking supply lines, such as [Marks & Spencer.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marks_%26_Spencer) [Samsung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samsung) said that it would ensure that previous controls ensured good work conditions under its code of conduct. [Apple,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_Inc.) [Esprit,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Esprit_Holdings) and [Fila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fila_(company)) did not respond[.\[179\]](#page-53-1)

The Chinese government is reported to have pressured foreign companies to reject claims of abuses.[\[180\]](#page-53-2) Apple was asked by the Chinese government to censor Uyghur-related news apps, among others, on its devices sold in China.[\[181\]](#page-53-3) Companies such as Nike and Adidas were boycotted in China after they criticized the treatment of Uyghurs, which resulted in significant drop in sales. [\[182\]](#page-53-4)

#### **Medical experiments**

Former inmates have said that they were subjected to medical experimentation[.\[183\]](#page-53-5)[\[184\]](#page-53-6)

## ![](_page_11_Picture_7.jpeg)

Posters in Bordeaux, France, accusing Nike of complicity in the Uyghur genocide.

#### **Organized mass rape and sexual torture**

From 2019 to 2021, [BBC News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BBC_News) and other sources reported accounts of organized [mass rape](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_rape) and sexual torture carried out by Chinese authorities in the internment camps.[\[185\]](#page-53-7)[\[186\]](#page-54-0)[\[187\]](#page-54-1)

Multiple women who were formerly detained in the Xinjiang internment camps have publicly made accusations of systemic sexual abuse, including rape, [gang rape,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gang_rape) and sexual torture, such as forced [vaginal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaginal) and [anal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anal_sex) penetrations with [electric batons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stun_baton)[,\[188\]](#page-54-2) and rubbing [chili pepper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chili_pepper) [paste](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paste_(food)) on [genitals.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genitals)[\[189\]](#page-54-3)[\[143\]](#page-47-4) Sayragul Sauytbay, a teacher who was forced to work in the camps, told the BBC that employees of the internment camp in which she was detained conducted rapes *en masse*, saying that camp guards "picked the girls and young women they wanted and took them away".[\[190\]](#page-54-4) She also told the BBC of an organized gang rape, in which a woman around age 21 was forced to make a confession in front of a crowd of 100 other women detained in the camps, before being raped by multiple policemen in front of the assembled crowd.[\[190\]](#page-54-4) In 2018, a *[Globe and Mail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Globe_and_Mail)* interview with Sauytbay indicated that she did not personally see violence at the camp, but did witness malnourishment and a complete lack of freedom[.\[191\]](#page-55-0) Tursunay Ziawudun, a woman who was detained in the internment camps for a period of nine months, told the BBC that women were removed from their cells every night to be raped by Chinese men in masks and that she was subjected to three separate instances of gang rape while detained.[\[190\]](#page-54-4) In an earlier interview, Ziawudun reported that while she "wasn't beaten or abused" while in the camps, she was instead subjected to long interrogations, forced to watch propaganda, had her hair cut, was under constant surveillance, and kept in cold conditions with poor food, leading to her developing [anemia.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anemia)[\[192\]](#page-55-1) Qelbinur Sedik, an Uzbek woman from Xinjiang, has stated that Chinese police sexually abused detainees during electric shock tortures, saying that "there were four kinds of electric shock... the chair, the glove, the helmet, and anal rape with a stick".[\[190\]](#page-54-4)

Chinese government officials deny all allegations that there have been any human rights abuses within the internment camps[.\[190\]](#page-54-4) Reuters reported in March 2021 that Chinese government officials also disclosed personal medical information of women witnesses in an effort to discredit them.[\[193\]](#page-55-2)

In February 2021, the BBC released an extensive report which alleged that systematic sexual abuse was taking place within the camps[.\[194\]](#page-55-3) The gang rapes and sexual torture were alleged to be part of a systemic [rape culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rape_culture) which included both policemen and those from outside the camps who pay for time with the prettiest girls[.\[186\]](#page-54-0) CNN reported in February 2021 about a worker and several former female inmates which survived the camps; they provided details about murder, torture and rape in the camps, which they described as routinely occurring[.\[195\]](#page-55-4)

#### **Outside internment camps**

#### **IUDs and birth control**

China performs regular pregnancy checks on minority women within Xinjiang.[\[10\]](#page-31-6) Some CCP officials have spoken about the "demographic imbalance" in southern Xinjiang; Liu Yilei, deputy secretary-general of the Xinjiang Production and Construction Corps CCP Committee, said that the "proportion of the Han population in southern Xinjiang is too low, less than 15 percent. The problem of demographic imbalance is southern Xinjiang's core issue."[\[196\]](#page-55-5)

Zenz reported that 80% of "new" Chinese [IUD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intrauterine_device) placements (defined in his study as total IUD placements minus IUD removals) in 2018 occurred in Xinjiang, despite the region constituting only 1.8% of the country's population.[\[197\]](#page-55-6)[\[198\]](#page-55-7)[\[199\]](#page-55-8) Assessing Zenz's analysis, [Xinjiang University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xinjiang_University) Professor Lin Fangfei argued that the appropriate measure is that 8.7% of IUD operations were performed in Xinjiang, adding that the Uyghur population growth was bigger than the Han population growth in the region[.\[200\]](#page-56-0)[\[201\]](#page-56-1)

Zenz reported that birth rates in counties whose majority population consists of ethnic minorities began to fall in 2015, "the very year that the government began to single out the link between population growth and 'religious extremism' ".[\[202\]](#page-56-2) Prior to the recent drops in birth rates, the Uyghur population had had a growth rate 2.6 times that of the Han between 2005 and 2015.[\[203\]](#page-56-3) According to Zenz's analysis of Chinese government documents, the Chinese government had planned to sterilize between 14% and 34% of childbearing-age married women in two predominantly Uyghur counties in 2019, while seeking to sterilize 80% of childbearing-age women in four rural prefectures in Xinjiang's south that are primarily inhabited by ethnic minorities[.\[197\]](#page-55-6)

According to a fax provided to CNN by the Xinjiang regional government, birth rates in Xinjiang fell by 32.68% from 2017 to 2018[.\[18\]](#page-32-5) In 2019, the birth rates fell by 24% year over year, a significantly greater drop than the 4.2% decline in births experienced across the entire People's Republic of China.[\[10\]](#page-31-6)[\[18\]](#page-32-5)[\[204\]](#page-56-4) According to Zenz, population growth rates in the two largest Uyghur prefectures in Xinjiang, Kashgar and Hotan, fell by 84% between 2015 and 2018[.\[12\]](#page-31-8)[\[205\]](#page-56-5)

According to Adrian Zenz, Chinese government documents mandate that birth control violations of Uyghurs are punishable by extrajudicial internment.[\[206\]](#page-56-6) Official records from [Karakax County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karakax_County) between 2017 and 2019 leaked to the *[Financial Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Financial_Times)* showed that the most common reason for detaining Uyghurs in camps was violation of family planning policies, with the second most common reason being for practising Islam. A 2018 Karakax government report said it had implemented "maximally strict family planning policies".[\[207\]](#page-56-7)

[The Heritage Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Heritage_Foundation) reported in 2019 that officials forced Uyghur women to take unknown drugs and liquids that caused them to lose consciousness, and sometimes caused them to stop menstruating.[\[173\]](#page-52-3) In 2020, an [Associated Press](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Associated_Press) investigation reported that forced birth control in Xinjiang was "far more widespread and systematic than previously known", and that Chinese authorities had forced IUD insertions, sterilization and abortions upon "hundreds of thousands" of Uyghur and other minority women.[\[10\]](#page-31-6) Many women stated that they were forced to receive [contraceptive implants.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Contraceptive_implant)[\[14\]](#page-32-1)[\[189\]](#page-54-3) The full scale of forced sterilization in Xinjiang is unknown, partly because of the Chinese government's failure to collect or share data, as well as the reluctance of victims to come forward due to stigma.[\[208\]](#page-56-8) The measures have been compared to China's past [one-child policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One-child_policy) targeting its Han population[.\[24\]](#page-33-3)[\[209\]](#page-56-9)

According to CNN, regional authorities do not dispute the decrease in birth rates but deny that genocide and forced sterilization is occurring; Xinjiang authorities maintain that the decrease in birth rates is due to "the comprehensive implementation of the [family planning policy.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Family_planning_policies_of_China)"[\[18\]](#page-32-5) The [Chinese Embassy in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Embassy_of_China,_Washington,_D. C.) said the policy was positive and empowering for Uyghur women, writing that, "in the process of eradicating extremism, the minds of Uygur women were emancipated and [gender equality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gender_equality) and [reproductive health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reproductive_health) were promoted, making them no longer baby-making machines. They are more confident and independent." [Twitter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twitter) removed the tweet for violating its policies[.\[93\]](#page-41-1)[\[210\]](#page-57-0)

#### **Forced cohabitation, co-sleeping, rape, and abortion**

Beginning in 2018,[\[211\]](#page-57-1) over one million Chinese government workers began forcibly living in the homes of Uyghur families to monitor and assess resistance to assimilation, as well as to watch for frowned-upon religious and cultural practices.[\[212\]](#page-57-2)

The ["Pair Up and Become Family"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Servant-Family_Pair_Up) program assigned Han Chinese men to monitor the homes of Uyghurs and sleep in the same beds as Uyghur women.[\[213\]](#page-57-3) According to Radio Free Asia, these Han Chinese government workers were trained to call themselves "relatives" and forcibly engaged in co-habitation of Uyghur homes for the purpose of promoting "ethnic unity".[\[212\]](#page-57-2) Radio Free Asia reports that these men "regularly sleep in the same beds as the wives of men detained in the region's internment camps."[\[214\]](#page-57-4) Chinese officials maintained that co-sleeping is acceptable, provided that a distance of one meter is maintained between the women and the "relative" assigned to the Uyghur home.[\[214\]](#page-57-4) Uyghur activists state that no such restraint takes place, citing pregnancy and forced marriage numbers, and name the program a campaign of "mass rape disguised as 'marriage'." [Human Rights Watch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Rights_Watch) has condemned the program as a "deeply invasive [forced assimilation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forced_assimilation) practice", while the [World Uyghur Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Uyghur_Congress) states that it represents the "total annihilation of the safety, security and well-being of family members."[\[214\]](#page-57-4)

A 37-year-old pregnant woman from the Xinjiang region said that she attempted to give up her Chinese citizenship to live in Kazakhstan but was told by the Chinese government that she needed to come back to China to complete the process. She alleges that officials seized the passports of her and her two children before coercing her into receiving an abortion to prevent her brother from being detained in an internment camp[.\[215\]](#page-57-5)

A book from [Chandos Publishing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chandos_Publishing) authored by Guo Rongxing stated that the 1990 [Barin uprising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barin_uprising) were the result of 250 [forced abortions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forced_abortion) imposed upon local Uyghur women by the Chinese government.[\[216\]](#page-57-6)

#### **Organ harvesting allegations and concerns**

[Ethan Gutmann](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethan_Gutmann)[\[217\]](#page-57-7) states that organ harvesting from prisoners of conscience became prevalent when members of the Uyghur ethnic group were targeted in security crackdowns and "strike hard campaigns" during the 1990s. According to Gutmann, organ harvesting from Uyghur prisoners dropped off by 1999 with members of the [Falun Gong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falun_Gong) religious group overtaking the Uyghurs as a source of organs.[\[218\]](#page-58-0)[\[219\]](#page-58-1)[\[220\]](#page-58-2)

In the 2010s, concerns about organ harvesting from Uyghurs resurfaced.[\[221\]](#page-58-3)[\[222\]](#page-58-4) According to a unanimous determination by the [China Tribunal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China_Tribunal) in May 2020, China has persecuted and medically tested Uyghurs. Its report expressed concerns that Uyghurs were vulnerable to being subject to [organ harvesting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organ_theft) but did not yet have evidence of its occurrence[.\[223\]](#page-59-0) In November 2020, Gutmann told *RFA* that a former hospital in Aksu, China, which had been converted into a Xinjiang internment camp, would allow local officials to streamline the organ harvesting process and provide a steady stream of harvested organs from Uyghurs[.\[224\]](#page-59-1) In a December 2020 *[Haaretz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haaretz)* article, Gutmann stated he believed at least 25,000 people were being killed in Xinjiang for their organs each year, claiming that "fast lanes" had been created for the movement of organs in local airports and [crematoria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crematoria) had recently built in the province in order to more easily dispose of victims' bodies[.\[217\]](#page-57-7)[\[225\]](#page-59-2)

In 2020, a Chinese woman alleged that Uyghurs were killed to provide [halal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Halal) organs for primarily Saudi customers. She also alleged that in one such instance in 2006, 37 Saudi clients received organs from killed Uyghurs at the Department of Liver Transplantation of Tianjin Taida Hospital. Dr. Enver Tohti, a former [oncology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oncology) surgeon in Xinjiang, thought the allegation was credible[.\[226\]](#page-59-3)[\[227\]](#page-60-0)[\[228\]](#page-60-1)

In June 2021, the Special Procedures of the United Nations Human Rights Council voiced concerns over having "received credible information that detainees from ethnic, linguistic or religious minorities may be forcibly subjected to blood tests and organ examinations such as ultrasound and x-rays, without their informed consent; while other prisoners are not required to undergo such examinations." The press release stated that UN's human rights experts "were extremely alarmed by reports of alleged 'organ harvesting' targeting minorities, including Falun Gong practitioners, Uyghurs, Tibetans, Muslims and Christians, in detention in China."[\[229\]](#page-60-2)

In July 2025, the Xinjiang Health Commission announced plans to expand the region's organ transplant centers from three to nine by 2030. Human rights experts and medical professionals expressed concerns, noting that Xinjiang's organ donation rate is 0.69 per million people, well below the national average of 4.6. [\[230\]](#page-60-3)[\[231\]](#page-60-4)

#### **Forced labor**

Throughout the [COVID-19 pandemic,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic) the Chinese government imposed forced labor conditions on Uyghurs. [\[232\]](#page-60-5)[\[233\]](#page-60-6) In January 2020, videos surfaced on [Douyin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Douyin) showing large numbers of Uyghurs being placed into airplanes, trains, and busses for transportation to forced factory labor programs.[\[234\]](#page-60-7) In March 2020, the Chinese government was found to be using the Uyghur minority as forced [sweatshop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sweatshop) labor. According to a report published by the [Australian Strategic Policy Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Strategic_Policy_Institute) (ASPI), no fewer than around 80,000 Uyghurs were forcibly removed from [Xinjiang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xinjiang) for purposes of forced labor in at least twenty-seven factories around China.[\[235\]](#page-61-0) According to the Business and Human Rights Resource Centre, a UK-based charity, corporations such as [Abercrombie & Fitch,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abercrombie_%26_Fitch) [Adidas,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adidas) [Amazon,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amazon_(company)) [Apple,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_Inc.) [BMW,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BMW) [Fila,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fila_(company)) [Gap,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gap_Inc.) [H&M,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H%26M) [Inditex,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inditex) [Marks & Spencer,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marks_%26_Spencer) [Nike,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nike,_Inc.) [North Face,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_North_Face) [Puma,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puma_(brand)) [PVH,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PVH_(company)) [Samsung,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samsung) and [Uniqlo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uniqlo) sourced from these factories[.\[12\]](#page-31-8)[\[236\]](#page-61-1) Over 570,000 Uyghurs are forced to pick [cotton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cotton) by hand in Xinjiang.[\[237\]](#page-61-2)[\[238\]](#page-61-3) According to an archived report from [Nankai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nankai_University) [University,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nankai_University) the Chinese [forced labor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forced_labor) system is designed to reduce Uyghur population density[.\[239\]](#page-61-4) In total, by 2021, the Chinese government had relocated more than 600,000 Uyghurs to industrial workplaces as a part of their forced labor programs.[\[234\]](#page-60-7)[\[233\]](#page-60-6)

A June 2025 report published by Global Rights Compliance found evidence linking 68 Western and international companies to forced labor in China's [critical minerals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Critical_raw_materials) industry, which is centered in the Xinjiang region.[\[240\]](#page-61-5) Xinjiang partly relies on forced labor transfer programs, which targets Uyghurs and other Turkic Muslim populations, and experts say Uyghurs are forcibly transferred to the mines or face punishment such as imprisonment, income loss, and harassment.[\[240\]](#page-61-5)[\[241\]](#page-61-6) Global Rights Compliance found 77 critical mineral sector companies operating in Xinjiang and identified 15 companies, including those outside China, actively sourcing from them. Some of the businesses had been flagged by the United Nations as being involved in state-run labor transfer programs. The report traced the XUAR mineral supply chains to various global brands, such as paint, thermos, aerospace, defense, nuclear tool, coffee, and soft drink companies[.\[241\]](#page-61-6)[\[240\]](#page-61-5) Regarding the report, spokesperson [Lin Jian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lin_Jian) for the [Ministry of Foreign A](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Foreign_Affairs_(China))ffairs of China responded that "China's Xinjiang has never forced anyone to transfer employment" and that it was a "complete lie" by "individual anti-China forces". He further added that individual organizations needed "to stop interfering in China's internal affairs under the guise of human rights and stop undermining Xinjiang's prosperity and stability."[\[240\]](#page-61-5)

#### **Outside China**

China has been accused of coordinating efforts to coerce Uyghurs living overseas into returning to China, using family still in China to pressure members of the diaspora. Chinese officials dismiss the accusations as fabrications[.\[242\]](#page-62-0)

China's robust surveillance system extends overseas, with a special emphasis placed on monitoring the [Uyghur diaspora.](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Uyghur_diaspora&action=edit&redlink=1)[\[243\]](#page-62-1) According to the *[MIT Technology Review](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MIT_Technology_Review)* "China's hacking of Uyghurs is so aggressive that it is effectively global, extending far beyond the country's own borders. It targets journalists, dissidents, and anyone who raises Beijing's suspicions of insufficient loyalty."[\[244\]](#page-62-2)

In March 2021 Facebook reported that hackers based in China had been conducting cyberespionage against members of the Uyghur diaspora.[\[245\]](#page-62-3)[\[246\]](#page-62-4)

Uyghurs in the United Arab Emirates, Egypt, Saudi Arabia and Turkey have been detained and deported back to China, sometimes separating families[.\[247\]](#page-62-5)[\[248\]](#page-62-6) CNN reported in June 2021 that "rights activists fear that even as Western nations take China to task over its treatment of Uyghurs, countries in the Middle East and beyond will increasingly be willing to acquiesce to its crackdown on members of the ethnic group at home and abroad."[\[247\]](#page-62-5) According to the Associated Press, "Dubai also has a history as a place where Uyghurs are interrogated and deported back to China."[\[249\]](#page-62-7)

A joint report from the Uyghur Human Rights Project and the Oxus Society for Central Asian Affairs found 1,546 cases of Uyghurs being detained and deported at the behest of Chinese authorities in 28 countries from 1997 to March 2021.[\[250\]](#page-63-0)

In 2025, reports emerged of [Chinese passports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_passports) containing false information issued by PRC consulates to Uyghurs living abroad in order to create a [pretext](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pretext) for deportation for possessing a "fake" passport[.\[251\]](#page-63-1)

### **Use of biometric and surveillance technology**

Chinese authorities use biometric technology to track individuals[.\[211\]](#page-57-1) According to Yahir Imin, Chinese authorities drew his blood, scanned his face, recorded his fingerprints, and documented his voice[.\[211\]](#page-57-1) China collects genetic material from millions of Uyghurs. China uses facial recognition technology to sort people by ethnicity, and uses DNA to tell if an individual is a Uyghur. China has been accused of creating "technologies used for hunting people."[\[252\]](#page-63-2)

In 2017, security-related construction tripled in Xinjiang. Charles Rollet stated, "projects include not only security cameras but also video analytics hubs, intelligent monitoring systems, big data centers, police checkpoints, and even drones."[\[253\]](#page-63-3)[\[254\]](#page-63-4) Drone manufacturer [DJI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DJI) began providing surveillance drones to local police in 2017.[\[255\]](#page-63-5) According to [ASPI,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Strategic_Policy_Institute) the [Ministry of Public Security](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Public_Security_(China)) invested billions of dollars in two government plans: the Skynet project (��⼯程) and the Sharp Eyes project (��⼯程).[\[254\]](#page-63-4) These two projects attempted to use facial recognition to "resolutely achieve no blind spots, no gaps, no blank spots" by 2020[.\[3\]](#page-30-1) A report by ASPI highlighted [Morgan Stanley's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morgan_Stanley) claim that, by 2020, 400 million surveillance cameras were to be operating[.\[254\]](#page-63-4) Chinese companies including [Sense Time,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sense Time) [Cloud Walk,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cloud Walk_Technology) [Yitu,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yitu_Technology) [Megvii,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Megvii) and [Hikvision](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hikvision) built algorithms to allow the Chinese government to track the Muslim minority group.[\[256\]](#page-63-6)

In July 2020, the [United States Department of Commerce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Department_of_Commerce) sanctioned 11 Chinese firms, including two subsidiaries of [BGI Group,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BGI_Group) for violating the human rights of Uyghur Muslims, by exploiting their DNA[.\[257\]](#page-63-7) BGI Group along with [Abu Dhabi-](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Dhabi)based AI and cloud computing firm [Group 42](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Group_42_(Emirati_company)) – accused of [espionage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Espionage) in 2019 – were named by the US departments of Homeland Security and State in an October 2020 warning issued to [Nevada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nevada) against the use of the 200,000 COVID-19 test kits donated by UAE under the partnership of G42 and the BGI Group. US intelligence agencies warned foreign powers who were exploiting patients' medical samples to dig into their medical history, genetic traits, and illnesses[.\[258\]](#page-64-0)

#### **Biometric data**

While he was Xinjiang Party secretary, Chen Quanguo launched "Physicals for All", purportedly a medical care program. "Every Xinjiang resident between the ages of twelve and 65" was required to provide DNA samples. Also collected were data on "blood types, fingerprints, voice-prints, iris patterns".[\[3\]](#page-30-1) Officials in [Tumxuk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tumxuk) gathered hundreds of blood samples[.\[252\]](#page-63-2) Tumxuk was named a "major battlefield for Xinjiang's security work" by the state news media.[\[252\]](#page-63-2) In January 2018, a forensic DNA lab overseen by the Institute of Forensic Science of China was built there.[\[252\]](#page-63-2) Lab documents showed that it used software created by

[Thermo Fisher Scienti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thermo_Fisher_Scientific)fic, a Massachusetts company.[\[252\]](#page-63-2) This software was used in correspondence to create genetic sequencers, helpful in analyzing DNA. In response, Thermo Fisher declared in February that it would cease sales to the Xinjiang region as a result of "fact-specific assessments"[.\[252\]](#page-63-2)

#### **GPS tracking of cars**

Security officials ordered residents in China's northwest region to install [GPS tracking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GPS_tracking) devices in their vehicles, allowing authorities to track their movements. Authorities said that it "is necessary to counteract the activities of Islamist extremists and separatists". An announcement from officials in [Bayingolin Mongol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bayingolin_Mongol_Autonomous_Prefecture) [Autonomous Prefecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bayingolin_Mongol_Autonomous_Prefecture) proclaimed that "there is a severe threat from international terrorism, and cars have been used as a key means of transport for terrorists as well as constantly serving as weapons. It is, therefore, necessary to monitor and track all vehicles in the prefecture.["\[259\]](#page-64-1)

### **Cultural effects**

#### **Mosques**

Mosques, Muslim shrines, and cemeteries in Xinjiang have been the target of systematic destruction[.\[3\]](#page-30-1)[\[260\]](#page-64-2) An estimated 16,000 mosques have been destroyed or damaged, minarets have been knocked down and "decorative features scrubbed away or painted over".[\[3\]](#page-30-1)

In 2005, [Human Rights Watch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Rights_Watch) reported that "information scattered in official sources suggests that retaliation" against mosques not sponsored by the Chinese state was prevalent and that the Xinjiang Party Secretary expressed that Uyghurs "should not have to build new places for religious activities"[.\[261\]](#page-64-3) The Chinese government prohibited minors from participating in religious activities in Xinjiang in a manner that, according to Human Rights Watch, "has no basis in Chinese law".[\[261\]](#page-64-3)

According to an analysis from *The Guardian*, over one-third of mosques and religious sites in China suffered "significant structural damage" between 2016 and 2018, with nearly onesixth of all mosques and shrines completely razed[.\[262\]](#page-64-4) This

## ![](_page_17_Picture_10.jpeg)

Mosque in [Tuyoq,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tuyoq_valley) Xinjiang (2005)

included the tomb of Imam Asim, a mud tomb in the [Taklamakan Desert,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taklamakan_Desert) and the Ordam shrine at the *[mazar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mazar_(mausoleum))* of [Ali Arslan Khan.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali_Arslan_Khan)[\[263\]](#page-64-5) According to *The Guardian*, Uyghur Muslims believe that repeated pilgrimages to these tombs fulfill a Muslim's obligation to complete the [Hajj.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hajj)[\[262\]](#page-64-4) In 2019, [Bellingcat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bellingcat) reported that "there is systematic repression and imprisonment of the Muslim Uighur minority in Xinjiang, and the destruction of cultural and religiously significant Islamic buildings in this province may be a further part of this ongoing repression.["\[260\]](#page-64-2) In the same year, Indonesian scholar [Said Aqil Siradj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Said_Aqil_Siradj) disputed that Uyghurs faced persecution, saying there was an increasing number of mosques being built and repaired in Xinjiang[.\[264\]](#page-64-6)[\[265\]](#page-65-0)

[Id Kah Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Id_Kah_Mosque) in Xinjiang is China's largest[.\[266\]](#page-65-1) [Radio Free Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_Free_Asia) reported that in 2018, a plaque containing [Quranic scriptures,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quran) that had long hung outside the front entrance of the mosque, had been removed by the authorities. Turghunjan Alawudun, director of the [World Uyghur Congress,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Uyghur_Congress) said the plaque was removed as "one aspect of the Chinese regime's evil policies meant to eliminate the Islamic faith among Uyghurs... and Uyghurs themselves"[.\[267\]](#page-65-2) Anna Fifield of *The Independent* wrote in 2020 that Kashgar no longer had any working mosques[.\[268\]](#page-65-3) *The Globe and Mail* reported that the only services at the Id Kah mosque, which had been turned into a tourist attraction, were staged to give foreign visitors the impression that religion was being practiced freely and that mosque attendance numbered only in the dozens.[\[269\]](#page-65-4)[\[270\]](#page-65-5) Indonesian outlet [Antara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antara_(news_agency)) released a video in 2021 documenting that 800 worshipers were in the mosque for Ramadan, but also that there was no [iftar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iftar) ritual due to pandemic restrictions.[\[271\]](#page-65-6)

Radio Free Asia reported that starting from early 2020, in response to international criticism, Chinese authorities started limited easing of religious restrictions in Xinjiang, reopening some mosques that were closed down[.\[272\]](#page-65-7) However, the broadcaster said that most Uyghurs have not returned to the mosques, fearful of their experiences in the previous crackdowns, and that Hui Muslims were given greater leeway than Uyghur Muslims[.\[272\]](#page-65-7)

#### **Education**

In 2011, schools in Xinjiang transitioned to what officials called a policy of bilingual education. The primary [medium of instruction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medium_of_instruction) is [Standard Chinese,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_Chinese) with only a few hours a week devoted to [Uyghur literature.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uyghur_literature) Despite this policy, few Han children are taught to speak Uyghur. [\[273\]](#page-66-0)

Uyghur students are increasingly attending residential schools far from their home communities where they cannot speak Uyghur[.\[274\]](#page-66-1) According to a 2020 report from [Radio Free Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_Free_Asia) (RFA), monolingual Chinese language education has been introduced in an influential high school in Kashgar that formerly provided bilingual education.[\[275\]](#page-66-2)
