Sayragul Sauytbay described how she was forced to teach at an internment camp, saying the camp was "cramped and unhygienic" with her detainee students given only basic sustenance. Sauytbay added that authorities forced

## ![](_page_18_Picture_8.jpeg)

Entrance to a school in [Turpan,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turpan) a Uyghurmajority city in Xinjiang, in 2018. The sign at the gate, written in Chinese, reads: "[You are] entering the school grounds. Please speak Guoyu ["the national language", i.e. [Mandarin Chinese\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandarin_Chinese)"

the detainees to learn Chinese, sit through indoctrination classes, and make public confessions.[\[171\]](#page-52-1)

In 2021, the standard Uyghur language textbooks used in Xinjiang since the early 2000s were outlawed and their authors and editors sentenced to death or life imprisonment on separatism charges. The textbooks had been created and approved by relevant government officials; however, according to the AP in 2021, the Chinese government said that the "2003 and 2009 editions of the textbooks contained 84 passages preaching ethnic separatism, violence, terrorism and religious extremism and that several people were inspired by the books to participate in a bloody anti-government riot in the regional capital Urumqi in 2009".[\[127\]](#page-45-3)

#### **Detained academics and religious figures**

In 2019 the [Uyghur Human Rights Project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uyghur_Human_Rights_Project) identified 386 Uyghur intellectuals who had been imprisoned, detained, or disappeared since early 2017[.\[276\]](#page-66-3)

Uyghur economist [Ilham Tohti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilham_Tohti) was sentenced to life in prison in 2014. [Amnesty International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amnesty_International) called his sentence unjustified and deplorable.[\[277\]](#page-66-4) [Rahile Dawut,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rahile_Dawut) a prominent Uyghur [anthropologist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthropologist) who studied and preserved Islamic shrines, traditional songs, and folklore, [disappeared.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disappeared)[\[278\]](#page-66-5)

RFA reported that the Chinese government jailed Uyghur [Imam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imam) Abduheber Ahmet after he took his son to a religious school not sanctioned by the state.[\[279\]](#page-66-6) They reported that Ahmet had previously been lauded by China as a "five-star" imam but was sentenced in 2018 to over five years in prison for his action[.\[279\]](#page-66-6)

## ![](_page_19_Picture_7.jpeg)

Uyghur economist [Ilham Tohti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilham_Tohti)

#### **Cemeteries**

In September 2019, [Agence France-Presse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agence_France-Presse) (AFP) visited 13 destroyed cemeteries across four cities and witnessed exposed bones remaining in four of them. Through an examination of satellite images, the press agency determined that the grave destruction campaign had been ongoing for more than a decade.[\[280\]](#page-66-7) According to a previous AFP report, three cemeteries in [Xayar County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xayar_County) were among dozens of Uyghur cemeteries destroyed in Xinjiang between 2017 and 2019. The unearthed human bones from the cemeteries in Xayar County were discarded.[\[281\]](#page-67-0)[\[282\]](#page-67-1) In January 2020, a CNN report based on an analysis of [Google](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Google_Maps) [Maps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Google_Maps) satellite imagery said that Chinese authorities had destroyed more than 100 graveyards in Xinjiang, primarily Uyghur ones. CNN linked the destruction of the cemeteries to the government's campaign to control the Uyghurs and Muslims more broadly. The Chinese government claimed that the cemetery and tomb destruction were relocations due to lack of maintenance and that the dead were re-interred in new standardized cemeteries[.\[283\]](#page-67-2)[\[284\]](#page-67-3)

This is all part of China's campaign to effectively eradicate any evidence of who we are, to effectively make us like the Han Chinese. ... That's why they're destroying all of these historical sites, these cemeteries, to disconnect us from our history, from our fathers and our ancestors.

—Salih Hudayar, whose great-grandparents' graveyard was demolished[\[281\]](#page-67-0)[\[282\]](#page-67-1)

Among the destroyed cemeteries is Sultanim Cemetery ( ), the central Uyghur historical graveyard with generations of burials, and the most sacred shrine in [Hotan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hotan) city, which was demolished and converted into a parking lot between 2018 and 2019[.\[285\]](#page-68-0) [China Global Television Network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China_Global_Television_Network) (CGTN), a Chinese state-owned international channel affiliated with the Chinese Communist Party, said that [37°07](https://geohack.toolforge.org/geohack.php?pagename=Persecution_of_Uyghurs_in_China¶ms=37_07_02_N_79_56_04_E_)′02″[N 79°56](https://geohack.toolforge.org/geohack.php?pagename=Persecution_of_Uyghurs_in_China¶ms=37_07_02_N_79_56_04_E_)′04″E

the graves were relocated.[\[286\]](#page-69-0)

#### **Marriage**

According to American journalist [Leta Hong Fincher,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leta_Hong_Fincher) the Chinese government offered Uyghur couples incentives to have fewer children, and for women to marry non-Uyghurs[.\[287\]](#page-69-1) According to the outreach coordinator for the U. S.-based Uyghur Human Rights Project,[\[288\]](#page-69-2) Zubayra Shamseden, the Chinese government "wants to erase Uighur culture and identity by remaking its women."[\[289\]](#page-69-3)

Marriages between Uyghurs and Han are encouraged with government subsidies. In August 2014, local authorities in Cherchen County [\(Qiemo County\)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qiemo_County) announced, "Incentive Measures Encouraging Uighur-Chinese Intermarriage", including a 10,000 CNY (US\$1,450) cash reward per annum for the first five years to such intermarried couples as well as preferential treatment in employment and housing plus free education for the couples, their parents and offspring. County CCP Secretary Zhu Xin remarked[:\[290\]](#page-69-4)

Our advocacy of intermarriage is promoting positive energy ... Only by promoting the establishment of a social structure, and community environment in which all ethnic groups are embedded in each other ... can we boost the great unity, ethnic fusion, and development of all ethnic groups in Xinjiang, and finally realize our [China dream](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Dream) of the great rejuvenation of our Chinese nation

In October 2017, the marriage of a Han man from [Henan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henan) Province to a Uyghur woman from [Lop County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lop_County) was celebrated on the county's social media page:[\[291\]](#page-70-0)

They will let ethnic unity forever bloom in their hearts, Let ethnic unity become one's own flesh and blood.

[University of Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Washington) anthropologist and China expert [Darren Byler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Darren_Byler) said that a social media campaign in 2020 to marry off 100 Uyghur women to Han men indicated that "a certain racialized power dynamic is a part of this process", commenting, "It does seem as though this is an effort to produce greater assimilation and diminish ethnic difference by pulling Uighurs into Han-dominated relationships.["\[290\]](#page-69-4)

According to RFA reports, in March 2017 Salamet Memetimin, an ethnic [Uyghur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uyghurs) and the Communist Party Secretary for Chaka township's Bekchan village in [Qira County,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qira_County) [Hotan Prefecture,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hotan_Prefecture) was relieved of her duties for taking her nikah marriage vows at her home.[\[292\]](#page-70-1) In interviews with RFA in 2020, residents and officials of [Shufu County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shufu_County) (Kona Sheher), [Kashgar Prefecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashgar_Prefecture) (Kashi) stated that it was no longer possible to perform traditional Uyghur nikah marriage rites in the county.[\[293\]](#page-70-2)

### **Clothing**

Chinese authorities discourage the wearing of headscarves, veils, and other customary Islamic attire. On 20 May 2014, a protest broke out in [Alakaga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alakaga) (Alaqagha, Alahage), [Kuqa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuqa,_Xinjiang) (Kuchar, Kuche), [Aksu Prefecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aksu_Prefecture) when 25 women and schoolgirls were detained for wearing headscarves. According to a local official, two died and five were injured when police fired on protesters. Subsequently, a *Washington Post* team was detained in Alakaga and ultimately deported from the region.[\[294\]](#page-71-0)

Documents leaked from the Xinjiang internment camps have noted that some inmates have been detained for wearing traditional clothing[.\[295\]](#page-71-1)

#### **Naming**

#### **Children's names**

RFA reported that in 2015, a list of [banned names](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naming_law) for children called "Naming Rules for Ethnic Minorities", was promulgated in Hotan, banning potential names including "Islam", "Quran", "Mecca", "Jihad", "Imam", "Saddam", "Hajj", and "Medina". Use of the list was later extended throughout Xinjiang[.\[296\]](#page-71-2)[\[297\]](#page-71-3) Legislation in 2017 made it illegal to give children names that the Chinese government deemed to "exaggerate religious fervor".[\[82\]](#page-39-6)[\[296\]](#page-71-2) This prohibition included a ban on naming children ["Muhammad"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_(name))[.\[296\]](#page-71-2)

## ![](_page_21_Picture_7.jpeg)

A Uyghur woman wearing a [hijab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hijab) in Xinjiang

#### **Village Names**

A report by NGOs [Human Rights Watch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Rights_Watch) and Uyghur Hjelp found that 630 villages in Xinjiang were renamed to reflect Communist party ideology and remove religious and cultural references. Examples include Aq Meschit ("white mosque") village being renamed to Unity village and [Dutar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutar) village being renamed to Red Flag village.[\[298\]](#page-72-0)[\[299\]](#page-72-1)

Commenting on the renaming, founder of Uyghur Hjelp, Abduweli Ayup, said that the Chinese government wants to "erase people's historical memory, because those names remind people of who they are"[.\[300\]](#page-72-2)

Acting China director at Human Rights Watch, Maya Wang, said that "The Chinese authorities have been changing hundreds of village names in Xinjiang from those rich in meaning for Uyghurs to those that reflect government propaganda [...] These name changes appear part of Chinese government efforts to erase the cultural and religious expressions of Uyghurs."[\[298\]](#page-72-0)

### **Classification of abuses**

Special purpose tribunals, scholars, commentators, journalists, governments, politicians, and diplomats from many countries have labeled China's actions variously as genocide, cultural genocide, ethnocide, settler colonialism, and/or crimes against humanity.

#### **Ethnocide or cultural genocide**

In 2008, Michael Clarke, an Australian terrorism scholar, noted that "there has emerged within the Uighur émigré community a tendency to portray the Uighurs as experiencing a form of 'cultural genocide' ", citing as an example a 2004 speech by [World Uyghur Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Uyghur_Congress) president [Erkin Alptekin.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erkin_Alptekin)[\[301\]](#page-72-3) In a 2012 *[Wall Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wall_Street_Journal) [Journal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wall_Street_Journal)* op-ed, Uyghur activist [Rebiya Kadeer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rebiya_Kadeer) described the CCP following "policies of Uighur cultural genocide"[.\[302\]](#page-72-4)[\[303\]](#page-72-5) In 2018, [UCL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_College_London) human rights scholar Kate Cronin-Furman argued in 2018 that the Chinese state policies constituted cultural genocide.[\[304\]](#page-72-6)[\[305\]](#page-72-7)

In July 2019, German academic Adrian Zenz wrote in the *Journal of Political Risk* that the situation in Xinjiang constituted a cultural genocide[;\[306\]](#page-73-0) his research was later cited by [BBC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BBC_News) [News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BBC_News) and other news organizations[.\[307\]](#page-73-1) James Leibold, a professor at Australia's [La Trobe University,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Trobe_University) called that same month the treatment of Uyghurs by the Chinese government a "cultural genocide", and stated that "in their own words, party

## ![](_page_22_Picture_4.jpeg)

## Pages from the China Cables officials are 'washing brains' and 'cleansing hearts' to 'cure' those bewitched by extremist thoughts."[\[308\]](#page-73-2)[\[309\]](#page-73-3) [\[310\]](#page-73-4) The term was used in editorials, such as in *[The Washington Post](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Washington_Post)*, at this point.[\[311\]](#page-73-5)

Since the release of the [Xinjiang papers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xinjiang_papers) and the [China Cables](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China_Cables) in November 2019, various journalists and researchers have called the Chinese government's treatment of Uyghurs an [ethnocide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnocide) or a [cultural genocide.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_genocide) In November 2019, Zenz described the classified documents as confirming "that this is a form of cultural genocide"[.\[312\]](#page-73-6) *[Foreign Policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_Policy)* published an article by Azeem Ibrahim in which he called the Chinese treatment of Uyghurs a "deliberate and calculated campaign of cultural genocide" after the release of the Xinjiang papers and China Cables[.\[313\]](#page-73-7)

In 2020, academic Joanne Smith Finley wrote that scholars, commentators, and lawyers had been increasingly referring to the human rights situation in Xinjiang as a genocide, rather than a cultural genocide. [\[4\]](#page-31-0)

#### **Genocide**

In April 2019, [Cornell University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cornell_University) anthropologist Magnus Fiskesjö wrote in *[Inside Higher Ed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inside_Higher_Ed)* that mass arrests of ethnic minority academics and intellectuals in Xinjiang indicated that "the Chinese regime's current campaign against the native Uighur, Kazakh and other peoples is already a genocide.["\[314\]](#page-74-0) Later, in 2020, Fiskejö wrote in academic journal *[Monde Chinois](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Monde_Chinois&action=edit&redlink=1)* that "[t]he evidence for genocide is thus already massive, and must, at the very least, be regarded as sufficient for prosecution under international law... the number of competent authorities around the world concurring that this is indeed genocide are increasing."[\[315\]](#page-74-1)

In June 2020, after an [Associated Press](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Associated_Press) investigation found that Uyghurs were being subjected to mass forced sterilizations and forced abortions in Xinjiang, scholars increasingly have referred to the abuses in Xinjiang as a genocide[.\[4\]](#page-31-0)

In July 2020, Zenz said an interview with [National Public Radio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NPR) (NPR) that he had previously argued that the actions of the Chinese government are a cultural genocide, not a "literal genocide", but that one of the five criteria from the [Genocide Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genocide_Convention) was satisfied by more recent developments concerning the suppression of birth rates so "we do need to probably call it a genocide"[.\[316\]](#page-74-2) The same month, law professor Ryszard Piotrowicz likewise wrote that the sterilization of Uyghur women and children constituted genocide under the 1948 Convention.[\[24\]](#page-33-3) [Chris Patten,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chris_Patten) the last colonial governor of [British Hong Kong,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Hong_Kong) said that the "birth control campaign" was "arguably something that comes within the terms of the UN views on sorts of genocide"[.\[317\]](#page-74-3)

Although China is not a member of the [International Criminal Court,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Criminal_Court) on 6 July 2020 the self-proclaimed [East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Turkistan_Government-in-Exile) [Turkistan Government-in-Exile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Turkistan_Government-in-Exile) and the [East Turkistan National Awakening Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Turkistan_National_Awakening_Movement) filed [a complaint with](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Turkistan_Government-in-Exile#International_Criminal_Court_case) [the ICC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Turkistan_Government-in-Exile#International_Criminal_Court_case) calling for it to investigate PRC officials for crimes against Uyghurs including allegations of genocide[.\[318\]](#page-74-4)[\[319\]](#page-74-5)[\[320\]](#page-74-6) The ICC responded in December 2020 and "asked for more evidence before it will be willing to open an investigation into claims of genocide against Uighur people by China, but has said it will keep the file open for such further evidence to be submitted."[\[321\]](#page-74-7)

An August 2020 *[Quartz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quartz_(publication))* article reported that some scholars hesitate to label the human rights abuses in Xinjiang as a "full-blown genocide", preferring the term "cultural genocide", but that increasingly many experts were calling them "crimes against humanity" or "genocide".[\[318\]](#page-74-4) In August 2020 the spokesperson for [Joe Biden'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joe_Biden)s presidential campaign described China's actions as genocide[.\[322\]](#page-75-0)

In October 2020, the [U. S. Senate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Senate) introduced a bipartisan resolution designating the [human rights abuses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights#Violations) perpetrated by the Chinese government against the Uyghur people and other ethnic minorities in Xinjiang as genocide[.\[323\]](#page-75-1) Around the same time, the [House of Commons of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Commons_of_Canada) issued a statement that its Subcommittee on International Human Rights of the [Standing Committee on Foreign A](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_House_of_Commons_Standing_Committee_on_Foreign_Affairs_and_International_Development)ffairs and [International Development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_House_of_Commons_Standing_Committee_on_Foreign_Affairs_and_International_Development) was persuaded that the [Chinese Communist Party'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Communist_Party)s actions in Xinjiang constitute genocide as laid out in the Genocide Convention[.\[185\]](#page-53-7) The 2020 annual report by the [Congressional-](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congressional-Executive_Commission_on_China)[Executive Commission on China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congressional-Executive_Commission_on_China) referred to the Chinese government's treatment of Uyghurs as "crimes against humanity and possibly genocide.["\[324\]](#page-75-2)[\[325\]](#page-75-3)

In January 2021, U. S. secretary of state [Mike Pompeo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Pompeo) officially declared that China was committing genocide against the Uyghurs and other ethnic minorities living in Xinjiang[.\[326\]](#page-75-4) This declaration, which came in the final hours of the Trump administration, had not been made earlier due to a worry that it could disrupt trade talks between the US and China. On the allegations of crimes against humanity Pompeo asserted that "These crimes are ongoing and include: the arbitrary imprisonment or other severe deprivation of physical liberty of more than one million civilians, forced sterilization, torture of a large number of those arbitrarily detained, forced labor and the imposition of draconian restrictions on freedom of religion or belief, freedom of expression and freedom of movement."[\[327\]](#page-75-5)

On January 19, 2021, incoming U. S. president Joe Biden's [secretary of state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Secretary_of_State) nominee [Antony Blinken](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antony_Blinken) was asked during his confirmation hearings whether he agreed with Pompeo's conclusion that the CCP had committed genocide against the Uyghurs, he contended "That would be my judgment as well."[\[328\]](#page-75-6) During her confirmation hearings Joe Biden's nominee to be the US ambassador to the United Nations [Linda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linda_Thomas-Greenfield) [Thomas-Green](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linda_Thomas-Greenfield)field stated that she believed what was currently happening in Xinjiang was a genocide, adding "I lived through and experienced and witnessed a [genocide in Rwanda.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genocide_in_Rwanda)"[\[329\]](#page-75-7)

The US designation was followed by [Canada's House of Commons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Commons_of_Canada) and the [Dutch parliament,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/States_General_of_the_Netherlands) each passing a [non-binding motion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-binding_resolution) in February 2021 to recognize China's actions as genocide.[\[30\]](#page-34-3)[\[31\]](#page-34-4)

In January 2021, the [United States Holocaust Memorial Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Holocaust_Memorial_Museum) initially stated that, "[t]here is a reasonable basis to believe that the government of China is committing crimes against humanity."[\[166\]](#page-51-3)[\[330\]](#page-76-0) In November 2021, the United States Holocaust Memorial Museum revised its stance to state that the "Chinese government may be committing genocide against the Uyghurs."[\[331\]](#page-76-1)

In February 2021, a legal opinion released by the [Essex Court Chambers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Essex_Court_Chambers) concluded that "there is a very credible case that acts carried out by the Chinese government against the Uighur people in Xinjiang Uighur Autonomous Region amount to crimes against humanity and the crime of genocide, and describes how the minority group has been subject to "enslavement, torture, rape, enforced sterilisation and persecution." "Victims have been "forced to remain in [stress positions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stress_positions) for an extended period of time, beaten, deprived of food, shackled and blindfolded", it said. The legal team stated that they had seen "prolific credible evidence" of sterilisation procedures carried out on women, including forced abortions, saying the human rights abuses "clearly constitute a form of genocidal conduct".[\[332\]](#page-76-2)[\[333\]](#page-76-3) The opinion identified three Chinese officials – President Xi, Chen Quanguo and [Zhu Hailun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhu_Hailun) – with whom the authors believed there was a "plausible" case that personal responsibility for the genocide lay[.\[22\]](#page-33-1)

On 13 February 2021, *[The Economist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Economist)* wrote that while China's treatment and persecution of Uyghurs is "horrific" and a [crime against humanity,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crime_against_humanity) "genocide" is the wrong word for China's actions due to China not engaging in mass murder.[\[334\]](#page-76-4)

According to a March 2021 [New Lines Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Lines_Institute) report that was written by over 50 global China, genocide, and international law experts[,\[143\]](#page-47-4)[\[335\]](#page-76-5)[\[144\]](#page-47-5) the Chinese government breached every article in the [Genocide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genocide_Convention) [Convention,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genocide_Convention) writing, "China's long-established, publicly and repeatedly declared, specifically targeted, systematically implemented, and fully resourced policy and practice toward the Uyghur group is inseparable from 'the intent to destroy in whole or in part' the Uyghur group as such.["\[336\]](#page-76-6)[\[337\]](#page-76-7)[\[145\]](#page-47-6) The report cited credible reports of mass deaths under the mass internment drive, while Uighur leaders were selectively sentenced to death or sentenced to long-term imprisonment. "Uyghurs are suffering from systematic torture and cruel, inhumane, and degrading treatment, including rape, sexual abuse, and public humiliation, both inside and outside the camps", the report stated. The report argued that these policies are directly orchestrated by the highest levels of state, including Xi and the top officials of the Chinese Communist Party in Xinjiang[.\[145\]](#page-47-6) It also reported that the Chinese government gave explicit orders to "eradicate tumours", "wipe them out completely", "destroy them root and branch", "round up everyone", and "show absolutely no mercy", in regards to Uyghurs[,\[145\]](#page-47-6)[\[144\]](#page-47-5) and that camp guards reportedly follow orders to uphold the system in place until "Kazakhs, Uyghurs, and other Muslim nationalities, would disappear...until all Muslim nationalities would be extinct".[\[338\]](#page-77-0) According to the report "Internment camps contain designated "interrogation rooms" where Uyghur detainees are subjected to consistent and brutal torture methods, including beatings with metal prods, electric shocks, and whips."[\[339\]](#page-77-1)

In June 2021, the Canadian Anthropology Society issued a statement on Xinjiang in which the organization stated, "expert testimony and witnessing, and irrefutable evidence from the Chinese Government's own satellite imagery, documents, and eyewitness reports, overwhelmingly confirms the scale of the genocide."[\[340\]](#page-77-2)

In June 2021, *[The New York Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_York_Times)* and [Pro Publica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pro Publica) published their analysis of over 3,000 videos, concluding that after the January 2021 U. S. declaration that China was committing genocide in Xinjiang, the Chinese government started an influence campaign featuring thousands of videos of Chinese citizens denying genocide and abuses in Xinjiang on Twitter and You Tube.[\[341\]](#page-77-3) In August 2022, the U. S. State Department published a report *PRC Efforts to Manipulate Global Public Opinion on Xinjiang* on the Chinese government's global efforts "to discredit independent sources that report ongoing genocide and crimes against humanity" in Xinjiang[.\[342\]](#page-77-4)[\[343\]](#page-77-5)

In an article for *[Ethnic and Racial Studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_and_Racial_Studies)*, David Tobin characterized China's actions as a "genocide by attrition". He wrote that China's actions against the Uyghurs, including sexual abuse, mass internment and physical and mental torture, were calculated to destroy "the foundations of [Uyghur] life, language, religion, and inter-generational cultural transmission, resolving the "ethnic problem" through social death of Turkic Muslims"[.\[344\]](#page-77-6)

A 2023 academic book by political theorists Alain Brossat and Juan Alberto Ruiz Casado labeled the accusation of genocide as unsubstantiated[.\[345\]](#page-77-7) They described the information used to apply the label as misleading and coming "exclusively from a few sources, for the most part overwhelmingly and openly partisan in their anti-China crusade"; they especially criticize Adrian Zenz's 2018 detainee study and 2019 sterilization study as "academically flimsy" and containing misleading or directly false claims, respectively. [\[345\]](#page-77-7)

Academics [Steve Tsang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steve_Tsang) and Olivia Cheung wrote that their research found no evidence that Xi Jinping advocates genocide against Uyghurs[.\[346\]](#page-77-8) Tsang and Cheung conclude that China's policies subordinate identity based on culture, religion, or minority language in an effort to establish a national identity based on Han heritage, language, and Xi Jinping Thought[.\[346\]](#page-77-8)

#### **Crimes against humanity**

In June 2019, the [China Tribunal,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China_Tribunal) an independent judicial investigation into forced [organ transplantation in](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organ_transplantation_in_China) [China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organ_transplantation_in_China) concluded that [crimes against humanity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimes_against_humanity) had been committed beyond reasonable doubt against China's Uyghur Muslim and Falun Gong populations.[\[347\]](#page-78-0)[\[348\]](#page-78-1)

The Asia-Pacifi[c Centre for the Responsibility to Protect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asia-Pacific_Centre_for_the_Responsibility_to_Protect) at the [University of Queensland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Queensland) concluded in November that evidence of atrocities in Xinjiang "likely meets the requirements of the following crimes against humanity: persecution, imprisonment, enforced disappearance, torture, forced sterilisation, and enslavement" and that "It is arguable that genocidal acts have occurred in Xinjiang, in particular acts of imposing measures to prevent births and forcible transfers."[\[349\]](#page-78-2) In December, lawyers [David Matas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Matas) and Sarah Teich wrote in *[Toronto Star](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toronto_Star)* that "One distressing present day example [of genocide] is the atrocities faced by the Uighur population in Xinjiang, China."[\[350\]](#page-78-3)

In 2021 the U. S. State Department's Offi[ce of the Legal Advisor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Office_of_the_Legal_Advisor) concluded that although the situation in Xinjiang amounted to crimes against humanity, there was insufficient evidence to prove genocide[.\[29\]](#page-34-2)

#### **Settler colonialism**

In addition to other classifications, some academics and researchers have also termed the abuses as part of an ongoing project of Han [settler colonialism.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Settler_colonialism)[\[351\]](#page-78-4)

### **View of discourse**

Writing in 2023, academic and former UK diplomat [Kerry Brown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kerry_Brown_(historian)) observes that the clash of labels between western and Chinese discourse on the issue of Xinjiang makes it nearly impossible to reach an empirical or neutral description of China's actions in Xinjiang.[\[352\]](#page-78-5)

According to American academic [Darren Byler,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Darren_Byler) discourses about Uyghurs in Xinjiang typically revolve around Uyghurs as either potential terrorists and resisters (from the view of the Chinese state) or objects of pity to be rescue (in western discourses), with little focus on Uyghurs as autonomous actors.[\[352\]](#page-78-5)

China contends that the [Gaza war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaza_war) demonstrates the hypocrisy of Western criticisms of China's treatment of Uyghurs.[\[353\]](#page-78-6) According to this view, if Western countries were genuinely concerned about human rights, they would focus instead on the negative humanitarian impact of Israeli military operations in Gaza.[\[353\]](#page-78-6) Following a 2024 statement by US Secretary of State Anthony Blinken which equated the suffering of Muslims in Xinjiang with the suffering of Muslims in Gaza, the Foreign Ministry of the PRC responded, "There is no conflict in Xinjiang, but the conflict is in the Gaza Strip. Muslims in Xinjiang are not suffering from hunger, expulsion, and killing, but the millions of Muslims in the Gaza Strip are suffering from hunger, expulsion, and killing. The US should stop playing double standards on human rights ..."[\[354\]](#page-78-7)

### **International responses**

#### **Reactions by supranational organizations**

#### **United Nations**

The UN High Commissioner for Human Rights (HCHR) began to discuss the possibility of a visit to Xinjiang with China in order to examine "the impact on human rights of its policies" in September 2020[.\[355\]](#page-79-0) Since then, the HCHR's office has since been negotiating terms of access to China, but the High Commissioner has not visited the country.[\[356\]](#page-79-1) In a February 2021 speech to the UNHRC, the Chinese Foreign Minister stated that Xinjiang is "always open" and the country "welcomes the [High](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_Commissioner_for_Human_Rights) [Commissioner for Human Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_Commissioner_for_Human_Rights) (HCHR) to visit Xinjiang".[\[356\]](#page-79-1) At a March 2021 meeting of the UNHRC, the United States ambassador condemned China's human rights abuses in Xinjiang as "crimes against humanity and genocide"[.\[357\]](#page-79-2)[\[358\]](#page-79-3)

## ![](_page_26_Picture_8.jpeg)

Protesters at the United Nations with the fl[ag of East Turkestan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flag_of_East_Turkestan)

China has turned down multiple requests from the UN HCHR to investigate the region.[\[359\]](#page-79-4) In January 2022, unidentified sources told the [South China Morning Post](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_China_Morning_Post) that UN rights chief [Michelle Bachelet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michelle_Bachelet) had secured a visit to Xinjiang, not to be framed as an investigation, some time during the first half of the year, as long as her office doesn't agree to the U. S. request of publishing its Xinjiang report ahead of the Beijing Winter Olympics.[\[360\]](#page-79-5) The visit occurred in May 2022[.\[361\]](#page-79-6) In a statement released by the UN, Bachelet said that she raised concerns in Xinjiang about the broad application of counter-terrorism and de-radicalisation measures (including their impacts on Uyghurs and other Muslim minorities) and encouraged the government to review such policies to ensure they fully comply with international human rights standards.[\[361\]](#page-79-6) Bachelet stated that while she was unable to investigate the full scale of the vocational educational and training centres (VETC), she raised with the Chinese government concerns about the lack of independent judicial oversight for the program, and said that the government provided assurances that the VETC system had been dismantled[.\[361\]](#page-79-6) U. S. rights advocates criticized Bachelet's visit as a propaganda victory for Beijing[.\[362\]](#page-80-0) The World Uyghur Congress and the Washington D. C.-based [Campaign for Uyghurs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Campaign_for_Uyghurs) called for her to resign[,\[363\]](#page-80-1) and Bachelet announced in June 2022 that she would step down from her role as UN human rights chief.[\[364\]](#page-80-2)

On 31 August 2022, Bachelet released a report on China's treatment of Uyghur Muslims and other Muslim minority groups in Xinjiang, the [OHCHR Assessment of human rights concerns in the Xinjiang Uyghur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OHCHR_Assessment_of_human_rights_concerns_in_the_Xinjiang_Uyghur_Autonomous_Region,_People%27s_Republic_of_China) [Autonomous Region, People's Republic of China.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OHCHR_Assessment_of_human_rights_concerns_in_the_Xinjiang_Uyghur_Autonomous_Region,_People%27s_Republic_of_China) The report found that China's treatment of these groups may amount to crimes against humanity. The report concludes that "serious human rights violations have been committed" in the province, which the report attributes to China's "application of counter-terrorism and counter-'extremism' strategies" targeting Uyghur Muslims and other Muslim minority groups. The report also said that "Allegations of patterns of torture or ill-treatment, including forced medical treatment and adverse conditions of detention, are credible, as are allegations of individual incidents of sexual and gender-based violence". China opposed the release of the report and claimed that it is based on "disinformation and lies". China also claimed that "All ethnic groups, including the Uygur, are equal members of the Chinese nation. Xinjiang has taken actions to fight terrorism and extremism in accordance with the law, effectively curbing the frequent occurrences of terrorist activities".[\[365\]](#page-80-3)[\[366\]](#page-80-4) On 6 October 2022, the UNHCR voted down a proposal to debate the alleged human rights abuses in Xinjiang[.\[367\]](#page-80-5)

#### **Reactions by country**

In December 2020, a case brought to the [International Criminal Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Criminal_Court) was dismissed because the crimes alleged appeared to have been "committed solely by nationals of China within the territory of China, a State which is not a party to the [Statute"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rome_Statute), meaning the ICC could not investigate them.[\[27\]](#page-34-0) In January 2021, the [United States Department of State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Department_of_State) declared China's actions as genocide[,\[28\]](#page-34-1)[\[29\]](#page-34-2) and [legislatures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legislature) in several countries have passed [non-binding motions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-binding_resolution) doing the same, including the [House of Commons of Canada,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Commons_of_Canada)[\[30\]](#page-34-3) the [Dutch parliament,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/States_General_of_the_Netherlands)[\[31\]](#page-34-4) the [House of Commons of the United Kingdom,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Commons_of_the_United_Kingdom)[\[32\]](#page-34-5) the [Seimas of Lithuania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seimas)[,\[33\]](#page-34-6) and the [French National Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Assembly_(France))[.\[34\]](#page-35-0) Other parliaments, such as those in [New Zealand,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_Parliament)[\[35\]](#page-35-1) [Belgium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belgian_Federal_Parliament)[,\[36\]](#page-35-2) and the [Czech Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senate_of_the_Czech_Republic) condemned the Chinese government's treatment of Uyghurs as "severe human rights abuses" or [crimes against humanity.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimes_against_humanity)[\[37\]](#page-35-3)

The [Uyghur Tribunal,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uyghur_Tribunal) a "people's tribunal" based in the [United Kingdom,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Kingdom) began to hold hearings in June 2021 to examine evidence in order to evaluate whether China's abuses against Uyghurs constitute [genocide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genocide) under the Genocide Convention[.\[368\]](#page-80-6)[\[369\]](#page-81-0)[\[370\]](#page-81-1) The tribunal was chaired by Geoff[rey Nice,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geoffrey_Nice) the lead prosecutor in the [trial of Slobodan Miloševi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trial_of_Slobodan_Milo%C5%A1evi%C4%87)ć, who announced the creation of the tribunal in September 2020.[\[368\]](#page-80-6)[\[371\]](#page-81-2) [\[372\]](#page-81-3) On 9 December 2021, the tribunal concluded that China has committed genocide against the Uyghurs via birth control and sterilization measures[.\[373\]](#page-81-4) The tribunal also found evidence of [crimes against humanity,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimes_against_humanity) torture and sexual abuse[.\[373\]](#page-81-4) The tribunal's final determination does not [legally bind](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Contract) any government to take action.[\[370\]](#page-81-1)[\[374\]](#page-82-0)

#### **Multinational corporations**

In reaction to the proposed [Uyghur Forced Labor Prevention Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uyghur_Forced_Labor_Prevention_Act) in 2020 to impose sanctions on "any foreign person who 'knowingly engages' " and require firms to disclose their dealings with Xinjiang,[\[375\]](#page-82-1) the president of the [American Apparel & Footwear Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Apparel_%26_Footwear_Association) said that blanket import bans on cotton or other products from Xinjiang from such legislation would "wreak havoc" on legitimate supply chains in the apparel industry because Xinjiang cotton exports are often intermingled with cotton from other countries and there is no available origin-tracing technology for cotton fibers[.\[376\]](#page-82-2) On 22 September 2020, the [US Chamber of Commerce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_Chamber_of_Commerce) issued a letter stating that the act "would prove ineffective and may hinder efforts to prevent human rights abuses."[\[377\]](#page-82-3) Major companies with supply chain ties to Xinjiang, including [Apple Inc.,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_Inc.) [Nike,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nike,_Inc.) [Inc.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nike,_Inc.) and [The Coca-Cola Company,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Coca-Cola_Company) have lobbied Congress to weaken the legislation and amend its provisions[.\[378\]](#page-83-0)

Xinjiang boycott advert on NYU's campus in New York, NY "Boycott Xinjiang Genocide Products!"

#### **2022 Winter Olympics Boycott**

In the aftermath of the 2019 leak of the Xinjiang papers which made public Chinese policies towards the Uyghurs, calls were made for a boycott of the [2022 Winter Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Winter_Olympics)[.\[379\]](#page-83-1) In a 30 July 2020 letter, the [World Uyghur Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Uyghur_Congress) urged the [International Olympic Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Olympic_Committee) (IOC) to reconsider the decision to hold the Olympics in Beijing[.\[380\]](#page-83-2)[\[381\]](#page-83-3) In a nonbinding motion in February 2021, the Canadian House of Commons called for the IOC to move the Olympics to a new location.[\[382\]](#page-84-0) The IOC met with activists in late 2020 about their request to move the Olympics[.\[383\]](#page-84-1) In March 2021, the [President](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_the_International_Olympic_Committee) [of the International Olympic Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_the_International_Olympic_Committee) [Thomas Bach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Bach) opposed a boycott, which would also damage the IOC image and finances,

## ![](_page_28_Picture_7.jpeg)

Uyghur human rights demonstration protest near the [White House,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_House) on 25 September 2015

and said that the IOC must stay out of politics.[\[383\]](#page-84-1) On 6 April 2021, a senior U. S. State Department official stated that the department's position "on the 2022 Olympics has not changed" and that it has not "discussed and [is] not discussing any joint boycott with allies and partners."[\[384\]](#page-84-2) Australia, Belgium, Canada, Denmark, India, Kosovo, Lithuania, Taiwan, the United Kingdom, and the United States announced diplomatic boycotts of the 2022 Winter Olympics[.\[385\]](#page-85-0)

### **Denial of abuses**

The abuses against the Uyghurs and related ethnic groups have been denied by the Chinese government. These denials have been both internal and external[.\[386\]](#page-85-1) The Chinese government has conducted propaganda campaigns on social media to further denial of the abuses. In 2021, the Chinese government posted thousands of videos to social media showing residents of Xinjiang denying claims of abuse made by Mike Pompeo; a joint investigation by [Pro Publica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pro Publica) and *[The New York Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_York_Times)* found the videos were part of an influence campaign coordinated by the CCP's [Central Propaganda Department.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Propaganda_Department)[\[114\]](#page-43-6) They have also used their existing disinformation networks, including [social media trolls,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internet_troll) to deny genocide and other human rights abuses against Uyghurs.[\[387\]](#page-85-2)

In 2020, during an interview with [Andrew Marr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Marr) of the BBC, the Chinese ambassador to the UK [Liu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liu_Xiaoming) [Xiaoming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liu_Xiaoming) denied any abuse against Uyghurs despite being shown drone footage of what appeared to be shackled Uyghur, and other minority ethnic, prisoners being herded on to trains during a prison transfer. The ambassador also blamed reports of forced sterilisations on "some small group of anti-China elements".[\[388\]](#page-86-0) In January 2021, Ministry of Foreign Affairs spokesperson [Zhao Lijian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhao_Lijian) responded to questions about the Uyghur genocide during a press briefing by stating, "China has no genocide; China has no genocide; China has no genocide, period."[\[389\]](#page-86-1)[\[390\]](#page-86-2) In February 2021, [Wang Wenbin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wang_Wenbin) called the Uyghur genocide the "lie of the century".[\[391\]](#page-86-3)[\[392\]](#page-86-4)

The abuses, and the existence of the camp network, have also been denied by a small minority of [American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Left) [left-wing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Left) media outlets. These include a left-wing blog called *LA Progressive* which began publishing denial articles in April 2020, while *[The Grayzone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Grayzone)* has been the most influential outlet to publish articles denying "China's ongoing repression of the Uyghur people".[\[393\]](#page-86-5) *The Grayzone* has been featured by Chinese state media, including CGTN and the *Global Times*. In 2020, Chinese government spokesperson [Hua Chunying](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hua_Chunying) retweeted a story published by *The Grayzone* which claimed to have debunked research into the internment camps in Xinjiang.[\[394\]](#page-86-6)

In February 2021, a *[Press Gazette](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Press_Gazette)* investigation found that Facebook had accepted money to promote content from Chinese [state media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_media) outlets such as *[China Daily](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China_Daily)* and [China Global Television Network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China_Global_Television_Network) that denied the mistreatment of Uyghurs[.\[395\]](#page-87-0)

According to anthropologist and China expert Gerald Roche, writing in *[The Nation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Nation)*, Xinjiang denialism only aids Chinese and American imperialism[.\[396\]](#page-87-1) He cited [Donald Trump,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Trump) who, according to former [National](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Security_Advisor_(United_States)) [Security Advisor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Security_Advisor_(United_States)) [John Bolton,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Bolton) believed that building internment camps was "exactly the right thing to do."[\[397\]](#page-87-2)

According to reports by the New Lines Institute, a think tank formally part of the [Fairfax University of](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fairfax_University_of_America) [America,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fairfax_University_of_America) [Ama Bhungane,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ama Bhungane) and *The New York Times*, [Neville Roy Singham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neville_Roy_Singham) funds a network of nonprofits and groups, including [Code Pink,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Code_Pink) that deny or downplay human rights abuses against Uyghurs and other Turkic Muslim minorities in Xinjiang[.\[398\]](#page-87-3)[\[399\]](#page-87-4)[\[400\]](#page-87-5) According to Sang Heae Kil of [San Jose State University,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Jose_State_University) within far-left circles, "there is a hesitancy to want to recognize that what's happening to the Uyghurs is a type of genocide."[\[401\]](#page-87-6)

In Taiwan, former [KMT chairwoman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chairman_of_the_Kuomintang) [Hung Hsiu-chu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hung_Hsiu-chu) received criticism for claiming that Western nations had "fabricated lies about the so-called 'forced labor' and 'genocide' in Xinjiang to undermine China's internal unity" while on a Chinese government-sponsored trip to Xinjiang in 2022[.\[402\]](#page-87-7)

[Darren Beattie,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Darren_Beattie) the acting [Under Secretary of State for Public Diplomacy and Public A](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Under_Secretary_of_State_for_Public_Diplomacy_and_Public_Affairs)ffairs during the [second Trump administration,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_presidency_of_Donald_Trump) previously wrote, "The Chinese aren't genocidal. They just object to Uyghur supremacy and uyghurness. If Uyghurs simply reject Uyghur supremacy, they'll have no problem functioning in Chinese society." In another post in 2021, he wrote "Uighurs don't like anti-Uighur racism, it must be because they are Uighur nationalists who think all of China is for Uighurs ONLY." He also wrote in the August 2024 that, "Britain treats its own native white people far worse than China treats its muslim Uighur population."[\[403\]](#page-87-8)

- Sintash, Bahram K. (October 2019). ["Demolishing Faith: The Destruction and](https://docs.uhrp.org/pdf/UHRP_report_Demolishing_Faith.pdf) [Desecration of Uyghur Mosques and Shrines" \(https://docs.uhrp.org/pdf/UHRP\\_re](https://docs.uhrp.org/pdf/UHRP_report_Demolishing_Faith.pdf) [port\\_Demolishing\\_Faith.pdf\)](https://docs.uhrp.org/pdf/UHRP_report_Demolishing_Faith.pdf) (PDF). Uyghur Human Rights Project. pp. 24–25. [Archived \(https://web.archive.org/web/20200911141130/https://docs.uhrp.org/p](https://web.archive.org/web/20200911141130/https://docs.uhrp.org/pdf/UHRP_report_Demolishing_Faith.pdf) [df/UHRP\\_report\\_Demolishing\\_Faith.pdf\)](https://web.archive.org/web/20200911141130/https://docs.uhrp.org/pdf/UHRP_report_Demolishing_Faith.pdf) (PDF) from the original on 11 September 2020. Retrieved 11 August 2020. "The Sultanim Cemetery has a history of over 1,000 years. King Sultan Satuq Bughra Khan of the Kara-Khanid Khanate (999– 1211) conquered Hotan (the Buddhist Kingdom Udun at that time), and spread Islam around 960 AD. During the conquest, four Kara-khan commanders, including Prince Sultan Kilich Khan, were killed and Muslims buried them at this location. Since then, the cemetery has been known as Sultanim Maziri (My Sultan Shrine) and became one of the most important cemeteries among Uyghur Muslims who have paid their respects here for over 1,000 years. In the center, the four commanders' graves were still there until China completely bulldozed the entire cemetery in 2019. Many religious leaders, scholars and other important people in Hotan's far and recent history have been buried in this cemetery."

- 291. Kang, Dake; Wang, Yanan (1 December 2018). ["China's Uighurs told to share beds,](https://apnews.com/9ca1c29fc9554c1697a8729bba4dd93b) [meals with party members" \(https://apnews.com/9ca1c29fc9554c1697a8729bba4d](https://apnews.com/9ca1c29fc9554c1697a8729bba4dd93b) [d93b\).](https://apnews.com/9ca1c29fc9554c1697a8729bba4dd93b) [Associated Press.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Associated_Press) [Archived \(https://web.archive.org/web/20191218031849/h](https://web.archive.org/web/20191218031849/https://apnews.com/9ca1c29fc9554c1697a8729bba4dd93b) [ttps://apnews.com/9ca1c29fc9554c1697a8729bba4dd93b\)](https://web.archive.org/web/20191218031849/https://apnews.com/9ca1c29fc9554c1697a8729bba4dd93b) from the original on 18 December 2019. Retrieved 18 December 2019. "In recent years, the government has even encouraged Uighurs and Han Chinese to tie the knot. Starting in 2014, Han-Uighur spouses in one county were eligible to receive 10,000 yuan (\$1,442) annually for up to five years following the registration of their marriage license. Such marriages are highly publicized. The party committee in Luopu county celebrated the marriage of a Uighur woman and a "young lad" from Henan in an official social media account in October 2017. The man, Wang Linkai, had been recruited through a program that brought university graduates to work in the southern Xinjiang city of Hotan. "They will let ethnic unity forever bloom in their hearts," the party committee's post said. "Let ethnic unity become one's own flesh and blood." "
- 292. [" 'No Sign' of Kazakh Imam Scheduled For Release From Prison in July" \(https://ww](https://www.rfa.org/english/news/china/kazakh-imam-08092017121527.html) [w.rfa.org/english/news/china/kazakh-imam-08092017121527.html\).](https://www.rfa.org/english/news/china/kazakh-imam-08092017121527.html) [Radio Free](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_Free_Asia) [Asia.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_Free_Asia) 9 August 2017. [Archived \(https://web.archive.org/web/20191216063112/http](https://web.archive.org/web/20191216063112/https://www.rfa.org/english/news/china/kazakh-imam-08092017121527.html) [s://www.rfa.org/english/news/china/kazakh-imam-08092017121527.html\)](https://web.archive.org/web/20191216063112/https://www.rfa.org/english/news/china/kazakh-imam-08092017121527.html) from the original on 16 December 2019. Retrieved 16 December 2019. "In March, Xinjiang authorities fired an ethnic Uyghur official for holding her wedding ceremony at home according to Islamic traditions instead of at a government-sanctioned venue. Salamet Memetimin, the communist party secretary for Chaka township's Bekchan village, in Hotan (in Chinese, Hetian) prefecture's Chira (Cele) county, was among 97 officials recently charged with disciplinary violations, according to an April 10 report by the state-run Hotan Daily newspaper. Local residents said the woman was relieved of her duties for taking her 'nikah' marriage vows in her own home. 'I think this may be a local policy unique to Xinjiang,' the source said. 'You have to first apply for a marriage certificate and then carry out the Islamic practice of nikah.' 'The imams aren't allowed to perform nikah if there is no marriage certificate, or they will be sent to prison.' "
